**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 654(b)(2) directs separation after an approved finding that the member stated that he or she is homosexual or bisexual, or words to that effect, unless a further approved finding establishes that the member has demonstrated that he or she does not engage in, attempt to engage in, have a propensity to engage in, or intend to engage in homosexual acts. Section 654(e) removes the processing obligation when the military determines both that the statement or conduct was intended to avoid or terminate service and that separation would not serve the military's best interest; it permits omission of processing, not a compulsory refusal to process. Those predicates are not established here.

Section 654(b)(1) separately addresses actual, attempted or solicited acts. Its retention exception requires all five findings: departure from usual behavior; unlikely recurrence; no force, coercion or intimidation; continued presence consistent with discipline, good order and morale; and no propensity or intent to engage in homosexual acts. These are not the rebuttal requirements of (b)(2). Section 654(b)(3), concerning marriage or attempted marriage to a person known to be of the same biological sex, is not a ground of this discharge. Neither separate route is adjudicated. The Court does not invalidate the directive's narrowing definition or decide an independent APA claim, procedural due-process claim or substantive-liberty claim.

The holding does not approve an irrebuttable status exclusion, every inference from identity, civilian exclusion, denial of rebuttal or retaliation for advocacy. No heightened orientation tier, actual-purpose proof requirement, individualized-impairment prerequisite or least-restrictive-means rule is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the judgment sustaining discharge, 5–4; no reinstatement, backpay, administrative rehearing or independent APA, procedural-due-process or substantive-liberty ruling.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Walmer supplies ordinary review and military prediction; its reserved statement question is resolved only at this application. Beach Communications, the separate Heller holdings and Romer preserve rational relationship, permissible generalization and the prohibition on bare hostility. Weiss supplies deference with independent review. Virginia’s sex-classification rule and Bowers’s conduct holding decide different questions and supply no automatic result here.

## Source: [state/holdings/civil-rights.md](../../../state/holdings/civil-rights.md)

#### Edwards v. Balisok

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1352.
**Decided:** 1997-05-19.

##### Procedural labels do not avoid necessary invalidity

**Holding:** In the in-custody review setting presented, damages and retrospective declaratory claims that a disciplinary adjudicator deliberately concealed exculpatory evidence and acted dishonestly or with disabling bias are not presently cognizable under §1983 when success necessarily establishes the invalidity of the outstanding deprivation of earned good-time credits, absent qualifying invalidation. The inquiry concerns the legal consequence of the actual claim, not its procedural label or omission of a request to restore credits; the reserved position of former prisoners and persons without a realistic lawful custody-review opportunity is not decided.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), unanimous. This direct agreement does not change Heck's earlier rationale alignments.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A later lawful hearing's possibility of the same result does not validate the existing adjudication. Present habeas availability is not made a universal element; former prisoners and persons without a realistic lawful custody-review opportunity remain reserved.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Heck's necessary-implication rule applies at its bounded in-custody scope; Preiser reserves credit restoration to habeas. Wolff and Carey preserve compatible process injuries, not categorical permission for every procedural challenge. Sandin and Young v. Harper preserve meaningful liberty and process without removing remedy-channel limits.

##### Genuinely independent procedural injuries require their own analysis

**Holding:** A claim falls outside Heck's invalidation prerequisite when its success does not necessarily establish that the credit adjudication was invalid, even if it arises from the same disciplinary hearing. A genuinely separable failure to state supporting facts can meet that boundary, and questioning or other procedural allegations must be classified individually; separability establishes neither a due-process violation nor entitlement to damages.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), unanimous. This direct agreement does not change Heck's earlier rationale alignments.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A nominal-damages label does not save an invalidity-dependent claim. Refusal of questions is neither invariably unconstitutional nor necessarily invalidating; Wolff permits refusal for irrelevance, lack of necessity, safety or correctional hazards. Ordinary pleading, personal responsibility, immunity, liability and damages requirements remain.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Heck and Wolff preserve genuinely independent injuries. Carey allows appropriate nominal relief for an established independent process denial without presuming compensable loss.

##### Qualifying invalidation precedes accrual; an exhaustion stay is not the remedy

**Holding:** For an invalidity-dependent claim within Heck's decided category, qualifying invalidation means reversal on direct review, executive expungement, invalidation by an authorized state tribunal, or a federal writ of habeas corpus calling the custody judgment into question, and the claim accrues only then. Without that event the claim is dismissed without prejudice rather than stayed to impose state-remedy exhaustion; failed review, exhaustion or expiration or loss of a review opportunity is not invalidation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), unanimous. This direct agreement does not change Heck's earlier rationale alignments.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The barred unaccrued claim consumes no limitations period while the prerequisite is unmet. No conversion to habeas or release follows. Independently required statutory administrative exhaustion and an independently authorized stay remain separate questions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Heck supplies all four invalidation routes, accrual and dismissal; Patsy bars general judicial state-remedy exhaustion, which differs from cognizability.

##### Prospective date stamping remains for ordinary equitable adjudication

**Holding:** A genuinely prospective request governing future date stamping of received witness statements ordinarily need not invalidate a completed credit adjudication and is not automatically barred by Heck. Because this request was not adjudicated below, remand requires examination of live standing, ordinary equitable requirements and the actual claim; no prospective label saves relief whose substance necessarily invalidates the past judgment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), unanimous. This direct agreement does not change Heck's earlier rationale alignments.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the categorical procedural exemption and remand unanimously. Dismiss invalidity-dependent damages and retrospective declarations without prejudice absent qualifying invalidation; separately classify genuinely independent reasons, questioning or other theories and adjudicate the unaddressed prospective request. No credit, damages, injunction or misconduct finding follows.

## Source: [state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md](../../../state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md)

#### Young v. Harper

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1598.
**Decided:** March 18, 1997.

##### Whether preparole community freedom is protected existing liberty

**Holding:** A prisoner released to live at home, work and participate in ordinary community life under parole-like conditions possesses conditional liberty protected by Morrissey. Legal custody, the preparole label, an overcrowding purpose or denial of formal parole does not dispense with revocation process.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), Parts II–IV, unanimous.

**Material application:** The August 1991 procedure cannot by itself establish a condition communicated at the October 1990 release or validate the March 1991 telephone return order. Silence in the earlier procedure does not establish all release conditions, prove compliance or make release irrevocable.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Five months is not a constitutional minimum. Formal parole entitlement, short furloughs, work details, ordinary institutional transfers and the later procedure’s prospective validity are not decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Morrissey protects freedom already conferred; Greenholtz concerns initial release and Meachum ordinary internal transfer. Sandin preserves direct constitutional liberty alongside its sufficient, nonexclusive state-entitlement route; no exhaustive program-entitlement finding is required.

##### Which preliminary and final safeguards govern withdrawal of that liberty

**Holding:** Revocation of this community liberty requires Morrissey’s preliminary and final protections, with their distinct qualifications. Initial arrest and detention need not await a completed final hearing, but final revocation must rest on constitutionally adequate process.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), Part III, unanimous.

**Material application:** For arrest and detention pending revocation, a reasonably prompt inquiry at or reasonably near the alleged violation or arrest determines probable cause or reasonable ground to detain for a release-condition violation. The decisionmaker must be uninvolved, but may be a different parole officer rather than a judge. Notice must identify the inquiry, its purpose and alleged violations; the participant may appear, speak, present relevant documents and witnesses and, on request, question adverse informants in person. An officer’s finding that identification would expose an informant to harm permits withholding confrontation of that informant. A summary must state the evidence and reasons without formal judicial findings; supported probable cause permits detention and institutional return pending final decision.

The State must offer a final hearing if desired, within a reasonable time after custody and before effective final revocation, to resolve disputed relevant facts and whether circumstances justify revocation, including denial and mitigation. Minimum safeguards are written notice of violations or grounds, disclosure of adverse evidence, a personal opportunity to present witnesses and documents, confrontation and cross-examination unless specifically withheld for good cause, a neutral and detached decisionmaker, and written evidence and reasons. The decisionmaker need not be a judge or lawyer. Informal letters and affidavits may be considered; matters already decided adversely in another forum, including a relevant conviction, need not be retried.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Preliminary informant-danger and final-hearing good-cause exceptions are separate permissions, not commands to withhold confrontation. No exception, waiver, dispositive admission, prior determination or supported probable-cause decision is established here. Morrissey’s two-month discussion establishes no universal deadline or safe harbor; no new evidentiary burden, automatic-release rule or separate counsel holding is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Morrissey’s flexible administrative safeguards govern in full, without converting revocation into a criminal trial or replacing it with a telephone instruction.

##### What relief follows from the failure to provide revocation process

**Holding:** The Tenth Circuit correctly reversed denial of habeas relief because this termination required Morrissey process. Oklahoma may still establish a legally available revocation ground through adequate procedures or rely on an independent lawful basis for custody.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Breyer), Part IV, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The procedural violation proves neither sentence expiration, formal-parole entitlement nor compliance with every possible condition.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirmance returns the case for proceedings under the protected-liberty and process holdings; it orders no unconditional release, release date or new state deadline. Permissible interim detention remains subject to the preliminary and final safeguards.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lynce supplies no unproved exhaustion defect or waiver; Gonzales’s concurrency construction supplies no authority for summary revocation.

For the eight-Justice accepted-rehearing holding in Young v. Harper, No. 95-1598, decided March 18, 1997, see Federal Courts, “Accepted rehearing and certiorari finality.”

## Source: [state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md](../../../state/holdings/criminal-procedure.md)

#### United States v. Lanier

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1717.
**Decided:** March 31, 1997.

##### What law must supply fair warning under 18 U.S.C. §242

**Holding:** Section 242, read with authoritative constructions, must have made the particular constitutional unlawfulness reasonably apparent when the defendant acted. Neither a later decision nor willful bad purpose can supply missing conduct-time notice, and novel or unforeseeable criminal enlargement is forbidden.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Moral reprehensibility and state criminality do not replace a definite federal right and notice. Vagueness, lenity, and the bar on unexpected retrospective enlargement retain related but distinct functions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Screws* supplies definite-right and separate willfulness requirements; courts construe the criminal statute rather than create common-law offenses.

##### Whether notice requires a Supreme Court decision on fundamentally similar facts

**Holding:** Fair warning may come from relevant pre-existing lower-court authority and from an obviously covered application of an established rule without a factual twin. Neither a Supreme-Court-only requirement nor a fundamentally-similar-facts prerequisite is lawful; conversely, an abstract liberty or bodily-security proposition does not settle an uncertain application.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** One lower-court decision or a fixed numerical consensus is not automatically sufficient. Actual conflicts, authority, particular context, and express reservations matter. No count's constitutional basis, notice, personal responsibility, or official nexus is resolved.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Elder* supplies examination of all relevant existing authority. *Anderson* requires particularized apparent unlawfulness, not repeated factual identity. *Pinder* and *Uhlrig* preserve unsettled-application and attribution limits; later holdings cannot supply earlier notice.

##### Whether the civil clarity standard substitutes for criminal elements

**Holding:** Criminal fair warning under §242 and civil clearly-established-law analysis use the same legal-clarity threshold: pre-existing law must make the constitutional unlawfulness apparent. The common threshold does not displace proof of deprivation, action under color of law, criminal willfulness, the criminal burden of proof, or any other statutory element.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Civil immunity analysis cannot itself convict, and judicial office alone does not prove that every act occurred under color of law. No categorically stricter criminal clarity standard is adopted.

##### Whether due-process rights or direct official assaults outside custody are categorically excluded

**Holding:** Appellate review may not exclude due-process rights from §242 categorically or treat the victim's lack of custody as automatic immunity for an official's own assault. Where a specific constitutional provision covers the claim, that provision governs; the Fourth and Eighth Amendments do not exhaust every potentially covered abusive official act.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), unanimous.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the en banc judgment and remand for count-specific consideration under the proper conduct-time notice rule and all remaining properly presented issues. The mandate does not reinstate convictions or sentence, order acquittal or retrial, or require a new evidentiary hearing.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal constitutional-assault formula or factual assault finding is made. Constitutional wrongdoing, personal participation, omitted protection, and supervisory attribution remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Screws* and *Price* preserve §242's due-process reach. *DeShaney*'s private-violence rule does not immunize direct official wrongdoing. *Graham* supplies the specific-provision rule. *Collins*, *Doe v. Taylor*, *Pinder*, *Uhlrig*, and *Nabozny* retain their distinct deprivation and attribution boundaries, without supplying notice retrospectively.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (1991)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 91-5052, 91-5111, 91-5166, 91-5167, 91-5244, 91-5246, 91-5307, 91-5331, 91-5332, 91-5401, 91-5416, 91-5476, 91-5583, 91-5594, 91-5692, 91-5730, and 91-5732.  
**Decided:** November 4, 1991.

##### Whether extreme repetitive frivolous filing permits denial of pending Rule 39.8 fee-waiver motions

**Holding:** When the Court is satisfied that pending submissions are frivolous and the applicants' repeated frivolous filings constitute extreme abuse of its process, Rule 39.8 permits denial of in-forma-pauperis status, meaning permission to file without paying fees. Denial may require payment of the docketing fee and compliance with ordinary petition form rules by a stated deadline; it does not decide petition merits or impose a broader prospective filing ban.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion joined by White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Souter; five of the eight participating Justices.

**Material application:** The present submissions were found frivolous; the applicants' extraordinary filing histories supported the finding of continuing abuse.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling is discretionary. It adopts no numerical threshold, does not make earlier denials alone conclusive, and decides no underlying claim or constitutional entitlement to unrestricted free filing.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The seventeen pending fee-waiver motions are denied, with an opportunity to pay docketing fees and submit petitions in the required form by November 25, 1991. Ordinary screening remains available upon compliance.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** In re McDonald and In re Sindram are applied as authority for tailored protection of judicial process; neither is extended to a categorical prospective ban.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, 504 U.S. 555 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1424.
**Decided:** June 12, 1992.

##### What evidence of Article III injury, causation and redressability is required at summary judgment

**Holding:** A plaintiff invoking federal jurisdiction must establish a concrete and particularized, actual or imminent injury, a causal connection fairly traceable to the defendant rather than independent third parties, and a likelihood that the requested relief will redress the injury. At summary judgment, the plaintiff must supply specific facts by affidavit or other evidence on the challenged elements; favorable reasonable inferences remain available, but allegations cannot substitute for the missing evidence.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; five Justices adopt the elements and litigation-stage evidentiary requirements. Stone concurs in the judgment without joining this section.

**Material application:** Earlier survival of the pleading stage does not establish the specific facts needed at summary judgment. The Court resolves this action on insufficient threatened personal injury.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The general redressability element controls. Its application to absent funding agencies and uncertain foreign-project responses appears only in the three-Justice Part III-B and is not controlling. Favorable reasonable inferences remain available; the decision does not replace Rule 56 or the separate APA programmatic-review holding of National Wildlife Federation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The merits declaration, mandatory rulemaking relief and dependent §1540(g)(4) fee award are vacated, and the action is remanded for jurisdictional dismissal. Six Justices support dismissal for lack of standing; seven support reversal, with Stevens relying on a noncontrolling statutory ground. No independent fee-entitlement rule or substitute award is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 (1975): applied for the plaintiff's personal stake and burden to establish standing; its statutory-rights discussion remains subject to concrete injury.
- Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737 (1984): applied for the constitutional elements and the limits on generalized judicial supervision.
- Simon v. Eastern Kentucky Welfare Rights Organization, 426 U.S. 26 (1976): applied for fair traceability and likely redress; no new majority application concerning these absent agencies follows.
- Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation, 497 U.S. 871 (1990): applied for the evidentiary distinction between pleading and summary judgment; its separate APA programmatic-review holding is not enlarged.
- Celotex Corp. v. Catrett, 477 U.S. 317 (1986), and Anderson v. Liberty Lobby, Inc., 477 U.S. 242 (1986): applied to require evidence of challenged essential elements while preserving genuine material disputes and reasonable inferences.

##### Whether past visits, indefinite return intentions and general wildlife interests establish imminent personal injury

**Holding:** Aesthetic, recreational and professional interests in observing wildlife can support standing, but these plaintiffs must show threatened interference with their own concrete use. Prior visits and an indefinite intention to return, without concrete plans or another supported basis for imminent renewed exposure, do not establish standing for these prospective claims; an ecosystem, animal or professional connection that supplies only general interest in distant species does not cure that defect.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III-A of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with five votes. Stone's separate concurrence supplies a sixth vote for the record-specific personal-injury conclusion without a formal opinion join.

**Material application:** The affidavits describe visits to Egyptian and Sri Lankan project areas but no sufficiently definite return undertaking or continuing activity showing imminent exposure. General ecosystem, animal and professional connections supply no missing evidence of these members' own threatened use.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No purchased-ticket or particular-document prerequisite is imposed. Actual use of an affected ecosystem and demonstrated injury transmitted across geographic distance remain undecided. Aesthetic, recreational and professional harm remains cognizable when it affects the claimant's own concrete activity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Jurisdictional dismissal leaves the statute's geographic reach unresolved and does not preclude a later action supported by sufficient standing facts.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Sierra Club v. Morton, 405 U.S. 727 (1972): applied; aesthetic injury is cognizable, but an organization's special interest cannot replace injury to a member.
- Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission, 432 U.S. 333 (1977): applied to require an injured member for this associational claim; no other associational requirement is changed.
- Los Angeles v. Lyons, 461 U.S. 95 (1983): applied for the distinction between past exposure and a present or imminent injury supporting prospective relief.
- Whitmore v. Arkansas, 495 U.S. 149 (1990): applied for actual or imminent rather than conjectural injury.
- Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation, 497 U.S. 871 (1990): applied to specific affected use; no categorical bar to geographically transmitted injury is established.
- Japan Whaling Association v. American Cetacean Society, 478 U.S. 221 (1986): distinguished because its whale-watching and studying members alleged an adverse effect on their own activities; concrete professional or ecological injury remains possible.

##### Whether ESA citizen-suit permission and an asserted procedural right replace threatened concrete injury

**Holding:** ESA §11(g)'s authorization for any person to sue does not give Article III standing to a plaintiff asserting only the public's common interest in lawful administration. A procedural right may protect a plaintiff's threatened concrete interest without requiring proof that the procedure will certainly change the ultimate agency decision, but the procedural label cannot replace that personal interest; these respondents have not shown the required connection.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; five Justices adopt the citizen-suit and concrete-interest rule. Stone does not join this section.

**Material application:** Section 11(g), 16 U.S.C. §1540(g), permits enforcement but identifies no concrete injury to these members independent of the unsupported future use. The public's common interest in lawful administration cannot supply the missing personal connection.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Congress may recognize concrete factual injuries previously inadequate in law; the outer reach of that power is not settled. Procedural protections tied to a threatened concrete interest remain cognizable without certainty of a changed ultimate decision. Neither §7(a)(2)'s geographic reach nor the binding effect of a revised rule on absent agencies is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The action is dismissed for want of Article III standing; no new consultation rule or cancellation of any foreign project is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Fairchild v. Hughes, 258 U.S. 126 (1922): applied to reject a general right to compel lawful administration as a sufficient personal injury.
- Massachusetts v. Mellon, 262 U.S. 447 (1923), and Ex parte Levitt, 302 U.S. 633 (1937): applied for the distinction between direct injury and an undifferentiated public grievance.
- United States v. Richardson, 418 U.S. 166 (1974), and Schlesinger v. Reservists Committee to Stop the War, 418 U.S. 208 (1974): applied to generally shared governmental grievances without distinctive concrete harm.
- Valley Forge Christian College v. Americans United for Separation of Church and State, Inc., 454 U.S. 464 (1982), and Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737 (1984): applied; a claimed right to lawful government conduct alone does not satisfy Article III.
- Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 (1975), and Sierra Club v. Morton, 405 U.S. 727 (1972): preserved for Congress's power to recognize injuries and broaden protected interests while retaining the plaintiff's own concrete injury.
- Trafficante v. Metropolitan Life Insurance Co., 409 U.S. 205 (1972), and Hardin v. Kentucky Utilities Co., 390 U.S. 1 (1968): distinguished as statutory recognition of concrete personal interests, not authorization of a wholly abstract grievance.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Johnson v. Jones

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-455.
**Decided:** June 12, 1995.

##### Factual sufficiency is not an immediate qualified-immunity appeal

**Holding:** A denial of qualified immunity is not immediately appealable under §1291 insofar as it determines only whether the pretrial evidence presents a genuine factual dispute about the official's participation in the alleged conduct. Mitchell's immediate appeal remains available for the separable legal question whether the conduct, on the relevant assumed facts, violated clearly established law; the present excessive-force participation dispute does not meet that condition.

**Proposition-level authority:** Justice Breyer's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; all nine adopt this bounded rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A proper legal immunity appeal uses the district court's assumed facts. If unstated, the appellate court may identify likely assumptions favorable to the nonmovant without deciding truth or reweighing sufficiency. An abstract legal contention does not bootstrap factual review. Genuine necessary connected review and future mixed-order questions remain at their reserved scope.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Cohen v. Beneficial Industrial Loan Corp., 337 U.S. 541 (1949): applies separateness as a cumulative collateral-order condition.
- Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511 (1985): retains immediate legal immunity review on assumed facts, bounded against ordinary factual-sufficiency appeals.
- Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct (June 6, 1994): applies category and cumulative-condition discipline; public immunity importance does not erase separateness.
- Swint v. Chambers County Commission (March 1, 1995): applies rejection of convenience-based appellate expansion; genuine necessary connected review remains at its reserved scope.
- Elder v. Holloway (February 23, 1994): preserves review of all relevant date-eligible legal authority on an authorized immunity issue; supplies no fact-reweighing jurisdiction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm dismissal of the reviewed immediate excessive-force factual-sufficiency appeal. The circuit's separate false-arrest immunity reversal remains undisturbed. No participation, beating, evidence-sufficiency, liability, state-law claim or other defendant's liability is adjudicated.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Demos v. Storrie, 507 U.S. 290 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-6846.

**Decided:** March 8, 1993.

##### The official-interference allegation is not defeated by filing history

**Holding:** Rule 39.8 permits denial of leave to file without prepayment only upon a finding that the particular petition is frivolous or malicious; a documented pattern of prior unsuccessful filings does not substitute for that finding. Demos's allegation that official interference prevented a timely appeal of the underlying prefiling order is a particular, non-conclusory claim that clears the nonfrivolousness threshold on this record, so the Court grants leave without deciding that the allegation warrants certiorari or would succeed on the merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices; all nine participating Justices adopt this filing-specific application and its limits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (November 4, 1991): applied as the existing discretionary, particular-submission rule; its extreme-abuse finding does not establish the frivolousness of a different current petition.
- Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals, No. 92-5584 (November 2, 1992): applied; its filing-specific frivolousness standard controls this later fee motion.
- In re Demos, 500 U.S. 16 (1991): distinguished; its narrower, earlier extraordinary-writ restriction addressed different filings and does not itself resolve this civil-certiorari fee question.
- Denton v. Hernandez, 504 U.S. 25 (May 4, 1992): its distinction between an arguable claim and a clearly baseless allegation informs this limited assessment without importing its statutory screening rules wholesale into Rule 39.

##### No prospective filing restriction on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a prospective fee-and-form condition on Demos's unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions because the established record does not show that individualized treatment of particular filings is inadequate. This case-specific refusal decides neither that a prior warning is invariably required nor that prospective restrictions are categorically unlawful.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices; all nine participate.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- In re McDonald, 489 U.S. 180 (1989): preserved as authority for targeted protection against extraordinary-writ abuse; no extension to all future civil-certiorari petitions is made here.
- In re Sindram, 498 U.S. 177 (1991): its extraordinary-writ setting is distinguished; its existing force is unchanged.
- Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals (November 2, 1992): applied; the same unanimous decline to impose a prospective restriction controls here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No finding establishes official interference, excuse of the unappealed default or merit in the underlying §1983 action. Existing extraordinary-writ restrictions are distinct; no universal warning requirement or numerical filing threshold is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Grant the fee motion and decline prospective restrictions. Denial of certiorari in the ordinary course supplies no merits precedent.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott, 506 U.S. 1 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-5584; No. 92-5618.

**Decided:** November 2, 1992.

##### No. 92-5584: an arguable current petition is not defeated by filing history

**Holding:** Rule 39.8 permits denial of leave to file without prepayment only upon a finding that the particular petition is frivolous or malicious; earlier unsuccessful filings cannot substitute for that finding. No. 92-5584's distinct general-rule and prospective statutory arguments clear the nonfrivolousness threshold on this record, so the Court grants leave without deciding that the arguments warrant certiorari or would succeed on the merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices; all nine participating Justices adopt this filing-specific application and its limits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (November 4, 1991): applied as the existing discretionary, particular-submission rule; its extreme-abuse finding does not establish the frivolousness of a different current petition.
- District of Columbia Court of Appeals v. Feldman, 460 U.S. 462 (1983): its general-rule/individual-judgment distinction supplies an arguable legal question here; the Court does not decide which side of that distinction the complete complaint occupies.
- Denton v. Hernandez, 504 U.S. 25 (May 4, 1992): its distinction between arguability and clearly baseless factual allegations informs this limited assessment; its statutory review and remedial rules remain unchanged and are not imported wholesale into Rule 39.

##### No. 92-5618: particular frivolousness and the available paid route

**Holding:** Where the current recusal petition identifies no concrete constitutionally disqualifying interest and develops no federal challenge to the independent state jurisdictional ground that defeats the action, those defects together establish frivolousness for this Rule 39.8 motion. In light of Martin's established extreme repetitive filing abuse, the Court exercises its discretion to deny this motion only, allowing payment of the Rule 38 fee and submission in Rule 33 form through November 23, 1992; the petition itself is not denied by this order.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of the per curiam opinion, joined by White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; six of nine control the motion, its stated ground and the compliance opportunity. Stone-Zsela, Blackmun and Stevens dissent from this part.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (November 4, 1991): applied to this distinct submission; fee denial remains discretionary and separate from disposition of certiorari.
- Aetna Life Insurance Co. v. Lavoie, 475 U.S. 813 (1986): distinguished because this petition establishes no comparable concrete personal financial stake; other constitutionally disqualifying interests are not categorically excluded.

##### No prospective filing restriction on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a prospective fee-and-form condition on Martin's unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions because the established record does not show that the available treatment of particular filings is inadequate. This case-specific refusal decides neither that a prior warning is invariably required nor that prospective restrictions are categorically unlawful, and imposes no condition on future civil or criminal filings.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of the per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices; all nine adopt the sufficiency of this narrower, record-specific ground without resolving the Court's power on a different record.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- In re McDonald, 489 U.S. 180 (1989): preserved as authority for targeted protection against extraordinary-writ abuse; no extension to all future civil-certiorari petitions is made here.
- In re Sindram, 498 U.S. 177 (1991): its extraordinary-writ setting and preservation of other filing categories are distinguished; its existing force is unchanged.
- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (November 4, 1991): its limited remedy is preserved; no warning, compliance default or further adjudication is inferred from that order.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No merits ruling on bar admission, ADA or Civil Rights Act coverage or retroactivity, immunity, the full complaint's jurisdictional status, or recusal follows. The two present defects in the companion operate together; neither professional affiliation, filing volume nor a state characterization alone establishes frivolousness. No universal warning prerequisite or categorical prohibition of prospective restrictions is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The lead fee motion is granted. The companion fee motion is denied with leave to pay the Rule 38 fee and submit a Rule 33 petition by November 23, 1992; that opportunity neither denies certiorari nor commands automatic denial upon passage of the date. No prospective restriction issues. The lead certiorari denial supplies no merits holding.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-757.

**Decided:** 1994-04-26.

For the other questions in Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 1994-04-26, No. 92-757, see Civil Rights — Section 102 does not add its new damages to this preenactment conduct; Civil Rights — The jury election depends on an available statutory damages claim.

##### Determine statutory temporal reach before applying the presumption against new retrospective consequences

**Holding:** When Congress has prescribed a civil statute’s temporal reach, courts apply that direction subject to constitutional limits; absent sufficient direction, they ask whether applying the provision would impair rights possessed when a party acted, increase liability for past conduct, or impose new duties concerning completed transactions. If it would, the provision does not govern those events without clear congressional intent; a pending case or reference to earlier facts alone does not establish such an effect, and prospective relief, jurisdiction, procedure and collateral matters require examination of what the particular provision actually changes rather than an automatic reopening of completed acts.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s general-framework portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg; five Justices adopt this organizing rule. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur on a distinct relevant-activity approach, not as additional joins at this level of generality.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Kaiser Aluminum & Chemical Corp. v. Bonjorno, 494 U.S. 827 (1990): applied to make statutory temporal direction the starting inquiry.
- Bowen v. Georgetown University Hospital, 488 U.S. 204 (1988): applied to the presumption against retrospective legal burdens; no automatic civil constitutional prohibition is inferred.
- Bennett v. New Jersey, 470 U.S. 632 (1985): applied to protect substantive obligations fixed under earlier law.
- Bradley v. School Board of Richmond, 416 U.S. 696 (1974): retained in its collateral litigation-fee setting and reconciled with the new-consequence inquiry, not overruled.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993): distinguished as judicial retroactivity; its equal-application rule remains unchanged.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule is provision-specific, not a declaration that the whole 1991 Act is inapplicable in pending cases. Section 402(a)'s enactment-date clause, §109(c)'s later-conduct limitation for extraterritorial amendments, and §402(b)'s exception for disparate-impact complaints filed before March 1, 1975 with initial decisions after October 30, 1983 remain distinct. Judicial retroactivity under Harper does not control legislative reach. An express congressional direction concerning the temporal operation of a statute resolves the threshold statutory question, subject to applicable constitutional limits. Such a direction does not alone determine whether Congress may constitutionally require reconsideration of an already completed Article III judgment. The present case concerns statutory consequences for earlier conduct in litigation properly before the courts and does not decide that separate final-judgment issue.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit judgment is affirmed, 8–1. No new damages trial, award or remand is ordered, and the existing equitable-remedy judgment and unreviewed constructive-discharge findings remain intact.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Day v. Day, 510 U.S. 1

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 92-8788, 92-8792, 92-8888, 92-8905, 92-8906, 92-9018, 92-9101, and 93-5430.

**Decided:** 1993-10-12.

##### No new prospective noncriminal-certiorari condition on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a new requirement that Day pay the Rule 38 docketing fee and comply with Rule 33's printed-form requirements for unidentified future noncriminal certiorari petitions because the established record does not show that treatment of particular filings under existing rules is inadequate. This record-specific refusal neither requires a prior warning in every case nor makes prospective restrictions categorically unlawful, and it does not decide any current fee motion or displace existing authority over demonstrated abuse.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion of the Court, supported throughout by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine participating Justices adopt the same rule, application, and limited disposition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott*, 506 U.S. 1 (1992), prospective-control portion: applies its record-specific refusal where treatment of particular filings has not been shown inadequate; neither a categorical prohibition nor a universal warning requirement follows.
- *Demos v. Storrie*, 507 U.S. 290 (1993), prospective-control portion: applies the same narrower treatment of unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions; no current fee outcome follows from that prospective rule.
- *Zatko v. California*, 502 U.S. 16 (1991): preserves discretionary denial on demonstrated current frivolousness and extreme abuse; its particular-submission remedy is not expanded into a future filing bar.
- *In re McDonald*, 489 U.S. 180 (1989): preserves its targeted extraordinary-writ authority and distinguishes that setting from the proposed future noncriminal-certiorari condition.
- *In re Sindram*, 498 U.S. 177 (1991): preserves its targeted extraordinary-writ holding without automatically extending it to these unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Filing volume establishes neither frivolousness of a particular petition nor inadequacy of particular-filing controls. No constitutional right to unlimited fee-free discretionary review or categorical rule about prospective restrictions is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The proposed new fee-and-form condition is declined. No current fee motion or underlying petition is adjudicated.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### In re Sassower, 510 U.S. 4

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 92-8933, 92-8934, 92-9228, 93-5045, 93-5127, 93-5128, 93-5129, 93-5252, 93-5358, and 93-5596.

**Decided:** 1993-10-12.

##### No new prospective noncriminal-certiorari condition on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a new Rule 38 payment and Rule 33 printed-form condition on Sassower's unidentified future noncriminal certiorari petitions because the established record does not show that treatment of particular filings is inadequate. The refusal imposes no universal warning requirement, filing-count threshold, obligation to exhaust every conceivable narrower measure, or categorical prohibition of prospective controls, and it decides none of the current fee motions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion of the Court, supported throughout by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine participating Justices support this component's stated ground and disposition. Direct unanimous procedural authority; no underlying certiorari or extraordinary-writ vote is inferred.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott*, 506 U.S. 1 (1992), prospective-control portion: applies its record-specific refusal of unsupported conditions on unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions; no categorical prohibition or universal warning prerequisite follows.
- *Demos v. Storrie*, 507 U.S. 290 (1993), prospective-control portion: applies the same individual-treatment approach without deciding any present petition's eligibility or merit.
- *Day v. Day*, prospective filing-control decision of October 12, 1993: applies its earlier same-day noncriminal-certiorari ruling within that ruling's actual scope; it supplies no finding about Sassower's papers.
- *Zatko v. California*, 502 U.S. 16 (1991): preserves discretionary responses to demonstrated current frivolousness and extreme abuse; its present-submission remedy is not expanded into a future restriction.

##### No new prospective noncriminal-extraordinary-writ condition on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a new Rule 38 payment and Rule 33 printed-form condition on Sassower's unidentified future noncriminal extraordinary-writ petitions because this established record does not demonstrate extraordinary-writ abuse and inadequacy of narrower treatment sufficient to justify the proposed scope. This refusal neither invalidates nor vacates existing restrictions, establishes that no other restriction exists, decides an underlying writ or current fee motion, nor weakens Rule 20, jurisdictional, criminal-proceeding, or habeas requirements; targeted controls remain available under their governing authority on a supported record.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion of the Court, supported throughout by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine participating Justices support this component's stated ground and disposition. Direct unanimous procedural authority; no underlying certiorari or extraordinary-writ vote is inferred.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *In re McDonald*, 489 U.S. 180 (1989): preserves its targeted extraordinary-writ authority; its established abuse setting does not itself establish the predicate for this new condition against Sassower.
- *In re Sindram*, 498 U.S. 177 (1991): preserves its extraordinary-writ holding without mechanically imposing its remedy on this different established record.
- *Day v. Day*, prospective filing-control decision of October 12, 1993: distinguishes its civil-certiorari scope; Day supplies no extraordinary-writ holding that controls this separate question.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The civil-certiorari and extraordinary-writ questions have distinct grounds. Day's civil-certiorari holding does not itself decide extraordinary-writ control. Both prospective questions had nine participants; the seven-Justice participation in current motion No. 93-5252 supplies no different prospective coalition or current fee holding.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both proposed new fee-and-form conditions are declined, separately. No current fee motion is adjudicated, and no existing restriction is vacated.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### In re Anderson, 511 U.S. 364

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-8312.

**Decided:** 1994-05-02.

##### Financial permission follows a particular assessment of the present paper

**Holding:** Rule 39.8 permits denial of financial leave when the Court is satisfied that the particular submission is frivolous or malicious; prior unsuccessful filings do not by themselves supply that finding. Anderson’s affidavit supports inability to prepay, and the present petition’s specific, partly corroborated access-to-review contention supports granting this motion despite substantial independent obstacles to the requested extraordinary writ; the grant establishes no entitlement to that writ.

**Proposition-level authority:** The per curiam opinion and order, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; nine Justices grant the motion on this bounded ground.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals: applied; the present-paper assessment preserves both its limited grant and its companion denial, rather than creating automatic leave for access claims.
- Demos v. Storrie: applied; an arguable, particular interference allegation can support fee permission without resolving the requested relief or default.
- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (1991): preserved; discretionary denial on a demonstrated present-frivolousness and extreme-abuse record remains available.

##### This record does not justify a new condition on all future extraordinary-writ filings

**Holding:** A prospective extraordinary-writ fee-and-form restriction must rest on demonstrated applicant-specific abuse and a supported connection between that abuse, the future category restricted and the inadequacy of individual treatment. This record does not establish those predicates for the proposed new condition; the refusal imposes no numerical threshold, universal warning requirement, duty to exhaust every imaginable lesser measure, or categorical prohibition of properly supported targeted restrictions.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same per curiam opinion and order, joined by all nine participating Justices: Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- In re McDonald, 489 U.S. 180 (1989); In re Sindram, 498 U.S. 177 (1991); and In re Demos, 500 U.S. 16 (1991): preserved as authority for supported, targeted extraordinary-writ restrictions; no categorical displacement occurs.
- Day v. Day and In re Sassower, decided October 12, 1993: applied within their actual prospective-control scopes; neither supplies a disposition of its unresolved current fee motions or automatically governs all custody-review applications.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No access allegation is accepted as proved. Financial permission establishes neither inadequate D.C. Code §23-110 remedies nor habeas jurisdiction, exhaustion, default excuse, a hearing, response order or relief from a conviction. In re Demos (1991) and Demos v. Storrie (1993) retain distinct extraordinary-writ and present-paper scopes.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Financial leave is granted and the proposed new prospective condition declined, each unanimously. No payment-cure deadline applies; no existing valid restriction is vacated or enlarged. Financial leave adjudicates neither the extraordinary writ nor its independent Rule 20 and habeas prerequisites.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### United States v. National Treasury Employees Union

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1170.
**Decided:** February 22, 1995.

For the other questions in this decision, see First Amendment — Public-employee honoraria restrictions (A broad prospective honoraria ban requires justification for its actual speech burden).

##### The existing ban is unenforceable throughout the certified class

**Holding:** Relief against the existing §501(b) prohibition extends throughout the certified class of affected Executive Branch employees below GS–16. The Court declines to create a general employment-nexus qualification by moving the series-specific parenthesis in §505(3) into the entire honorarium definition; this class remedy does not establish that every class payment is unrelated or that a law separately and validly restricting connected payments would fail.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part II, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Pickering remains the merits framework rather than authority to rewrite statutory coverage. Simon & Schuster and Turner retain their stated financial-burden and evidentiary principles; neither compels a judicially created nexus statute. The remedy preserves the independent force of valid ethics laws and decides no new constitutional rule for congressional or judicial employees.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 505(3)'s series-specific nexus qualification and the travel and charitable-payment conditions are set out with this decision's First Amendment holding. The class remedy does not rewrite those conditions or extend to §501(a)'s distinct outside-income ceiling.

##### The injunction does not adjudicate the claims of unrepresented senior employees

**Holding:** The injunction must be confined to the represented class and the individually represented claim retained here; it may not extend this adjudication to unrepresented Executive employees at GS–16 or above. Removing that nonparty protection does not hold their claims meritless or decide materially different applications of the honoraria ban.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part III-A, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen v. Women's Health Center, June 30, 1994, supplies attention to actual persons, claims and the lawful scope of remedial adjustment; it is applied without importing its different clinic facts or an automatic bar on all broader relief in other cases.

##### Crane retains his individual relief without a grade-only remand

**Holding:** Peter G. Crane's existing individual relief is retained; his GS–16 status and resulting exclusion from the certified class do not alone require its removal or a separate remand. This disposition concerns a named respondent under the Government's alternative respondents-plus-class request, not a general constitutional exemption for senior employees or a substantive concession of Crane's entire merits case.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part III-B, joined by Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices; Stone-Zsela adds a judgment-only sixth vote for Crane's relief.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Madsen's bounded remedial treatment is applied to distinguish the necessary removal of nonparty protection from an unsupported grade-only reopening of an individual judgment. No new universal appellate-waiver rule or general senior-employee First Amendment exemption is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part and remanded. Nonenforcement of the existing ban throughout the certified below-GS–16 class is preserved by five votes; Crane retains individual relief by six; relief for unrepresented senior employees is removed. Congressional and judicial coverage, §501(a)'s outside-income ceiling and valid independent ethics restrictions are not invalidated. No penalty or compensation award is entered.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Garlotte v. Fordice

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-6790.
**Decided:** May 30, 1995.

##### Custody under a continuous consecutive series

**Holding:** A prisoner serving a continuous unexpired series of consecutive sentences remains in custody under the earlier conviction when invalidating it would advance release from the aggregate term, although its first segment has been served. This establishes the custody threshold, leaving the conviction’s validity and remaining procedural and merits conditions for review.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Breyer; eight Justices directly support this rule.

**Limits and questions not reached:** An independently expired sentence's collateral consequences alone remain insufficient under Maleng. The rule concerns a continuous unexpired aggregate whose release date would advance; ordinary habeas procedural and merits requirements remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Peyton v. Rowe, 391 U.S. 54: extends its aggregate treatment from a future segment to a completed first segment of a still-unexpired consecutive series when invalidation advances release.
- Maleng v. Cook, 490 U.S. 488: distinguishes a wholly expired independent sentence and preserves the rule that collateral consequences alone do not supply custody under that conviction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Fifth Circuit's jurisdictional dismissal and remand for remaining procedural and merits review, including review of the district court's merits denial. No conviction is invalidated, writ awarded or immediate release ordered.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Behrens v. Pelletier, 516 U.S. 299

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1244.

**Decided:** February 21, 1996.

##### A prior appeal does not categorically exhaust legal-immunity review

**Holding:** An earlier pleading-stage qualified-immunity appeal does not, merely because it occurred, bar a later summary-judgment appeal presenting a separable legal immunity question that otherwise satisfies the collateral-order requirements. The later appeal may address legal immunity on properly assumed facts; this rule authorizes neither factual-sufficiency review nor repeated appeals of an unchanged legal ruling or matters resolved by the first mandate.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; seven Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Cohen's cumulative conditions and Mitchell's legal-immunity category apply. Digital Equipment's importance and category discipline remains; Johnson supplies no numerical ceiling and retains its factual boundary.

##### An authorized appeal uses assumed facts and excludes factual sufficiency

**Holding:** Within an otherwise authorized qualified-immunity appeal, the appellate court may decide whether conduct on the District Court's properly assumed or sufficiently supported facts violates clearly established law, but may not choose which factual account is true or reweigh whether evidence supports it. If the relevant assumptions are unstated, the court may identify the likely assumptions favorable to the nonmovant in the manner Johnson permits, without deciding truth or evidentiary sufficiency.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part II, unanimous. Stevens and Breyer condition agreement on an otherwise authorized appeal and do not find successive authorization here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Johnson and Kimberlin preserve assumed-fact review without factual selection. Elder requires relevant conduct-date law but creates no jurisdiction or retrospective notice. Swint rejects convenience-based expansion; Doe's factual-sufficiency exclusion remains.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No immunity, constitutional violation, property interest, factual truth, evidentiary sufficiency or damages issue is decided.

##### A different surviving claim does not itself defeat a qualifying immunity appeal

**Holding:** The fact that an official must still defend another claim does not, by itself, eliminate an otherwise authorized immediate appeal of a separable legal immunity denial on a different claim. That claim-specific principle neither supplies missing appellate authorization nor makes the other claim reviewable.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's Part III, joined by all seven other Associate Justices; eight Justices. Stone-Zsela does not join; Stevens and Breyer continue to oppose successive appeal as of right.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Mitchell protects against defending the claim to which immunity applies; Swint prevents a proper appeal from carrying unrelated matters and leaves its necessary-connected-review reservation open.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse only the categorical Ninth Circuit dismissal and remand to identify proper assumptions favorable to the nonmovant and any actually reviewable legal issue, dismissing factual-sufficiency and otherwise unavailable issues. The summary-judgment denial is not reversed, immunity is not awarded, and neither the separate United States negligence claim nor earlier substitution ruling is reopened. No stay or permission is granted.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Peacock v. Thomas, 516 U.S. 349

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1453.

**Decided:** February 21, 1996.

##### Whether the later collection action has original ERISA jurisdiction

**Holding:** A later action imposing the corporate judgment on Peacock alleges no underlying ERISA or plan violation supporting the asserted federal jurisdiction. Veil piercing is a means of imposing liability, not an independent statutory violation; §502(a)(3) supplies relief for the obligations it identifies, not equitable relief at large.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices. Stone-Zsela does not join this analysis.

**Material application:** Later control and collection obstruction do not erase the original nonfiduciary adjudication or establish fiduciary responsibility during the earlier breaches. An unrestricted compensation demand does not become an authorized remedy merely by being called equitable.

**Limits and questions not reached:** ERISA's possible permission for veil piercing in an appropriately founded action is assumed without decision. No general ban on equitable monetary remedies or rule treating every unsuccessful federal merits theory as jurisdictionally defective follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Mertens's bounded equitable-category holding and antecedent-action reservation remain. O'Melveny and Travelers supply no general federal collection rule, and the lower court's categorical federal-preemption rationale is not approved.

##### Whether ancillary enforcement jurisdiction permits a new full-debt action

**Holding:** Authority to identify, preserve and execute against a judgment debtor's assets does not authorize a later independent action imposing its entire debt on another person without an independent jurisdictional basis. Prior party status does not itself make that person a judgment debtor.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices.

**Material application:** The later conduct differs materially from the original pension violations and requires new facts, duties and defenses. Rule 69 supplies procedure, not original jurisdiction; the original 1987 action predates §1367's December 1, 1990 boundary, and the later case supplies no original claim to which supplemental jurisdiction can attach.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Contempt, asset discovery, ordinary attachment and garnishment, fraudulent-transfer process directed to debtor property and independently founded actions remain available. No particular asset restoration, general alter-ego prohibition, personal-jurisdiction or service holding, governing South Carolina enforcement rule, federal displacement or later-conduct preclusion determination is made. The preserved service objection is neither rejected nor waived; subject-matter jurisdiction alone supplies no nationwide service.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for dismissal of the later action and removal of dependent personal liability, interest, fees and expenses, including the appellate recalculation instruction. The vacated $205,182.01 combined assessment is not reinstated; the original $187,628.93 Tru-Tech judgment remains intact. Otherwise available state-law rights and independently founded actions are not extinguished.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Kokkonen's independent-authority requirement and distinct decree-effectuation branch apply; Beecher limits creation of new obligations. Riggs and Labette County are distinguished because they enforced existing official duties rather than shifting the judgment debt.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1809.

**Decided:** February 27, 1996.

##### Whether §1738 reaches an incorporated class-settlement release of exclusively federal claims

**Holding:** A state judicial judgment incorporating a class settlement falls within §1738 even when it releases claims subject to exclusive federal adjudicatory jurisdiction. Recognition begins with rendering-state law; class form, consent disposition and exclusive jurisdiction alone create no categorical exemption.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A representative's authority to bind absent members differs from an owner's personal settlement capacity. Silence after notice is not individual contractual consent; release wording supplies no missing constitutional representation. State courts acquire no power to adjudicate Exchange Act liability.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Marrese requires state-law inquiry and preserves its distinct settlement-release reservation; its adjudicated-claim competency limit is not mechanically transferred to releases. Kokkonen's incorporation distinction does not make representative authority self-validating.

##### The ordinary Delaware effect of this judgment

**Holding:** The affirmed Delaware judgment's incorporated release receives ordinary Delaware preclusive effect and cannot lose that effect merely because a federal litigant alleges state-law error in settlement approval, subject to unresolved federal constitutional binding limits.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer; seven Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Nottingham's identical-factual-predicate and representative limits and Prezant's Rule 23 and express adequacy requirements remain. No same-acquisition universal release rule, finding about counsel incentives or full-and-fair adequacy determination is made.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Marrese supplies the inquiry, not an inflexible first-instance remand. Kremer preserves finality subject to due process and the actual scope of matters determined.

##### Whether Exchange Act §27 impliedly displaces §1738 for this category

**Holding:** Section 27 does not impliedly partially repeal §1738 for the state settlement judgments at issue. Exclusive federal adjudication of Exchange Act liability is compatible with a state court's approval of an authorized representative release within an action over which it has jurisdiction.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer; eight Justices. Ginsburg reserves this further determination.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Claim valuation is distinct from adjudicating federal liability. Recognition does not approve every state injunction or contempt remedy interfering with a pending federal action; an independently preserved coercive-operation objection retains its own lawful treatment.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Marrese and Kremer require a supported basis to displace recognition; uniform federal adjudication does not establish statutory incompatibility here.

##### Constitutional representation and the reserved scope of collateral review

**Holding:** Section 1738 cannot bind an absent class member through constitutionally inadequate representation; notice, objection opportunities and opt-out rights do not independently cure that defect. Actual constitutional adequacy and binding effect remain undecided, so immediate dismissal is not directed.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part IV-A, unanimous. Part IV-B's reservation of the complete permissible collateral-review scope, including the claimed full-and-fair adjudication of adequacy, has the eight Associate Justices' joins; Stone-Zsela's structural-failure standard remains separate and noncontrolling.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The state findings, discovery and adversarial hearing are not erased; neither a recital nor criticism conclusively resolves constitutional adequacy. The remand does not authorize unrestricted relitigation. No tender-offer liability, §§14(d)(6)–(7) private right, new class, damages or independent contract-consent theory is decided; valid opt-outs remain excluded.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse, 7–2, and remand for properly presented unresolved constitutional and binding objections and their antecedent collateral availability, consistently with ordinary Delaware effect and §27. Covered claims may be dismissed only after remaining legally available grounds are resolved; no immediate dismissal or wholesale settlement invalidation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Hansberry and Shutts require adequate representation distinct from notice and exclusion. Peacock's new-debtor authority limit and Central Bank's confined affirmative-assistance securities remedy remain distinct and supply neither a new cause nor claim value here.

## Source: [state/holdings/federal-courts.md](../../../state/holdings/federal-courts.md)

#### Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-386.
**Decided:** June 10, 1996.

##### Whether the dispositive nonparty bar is final for limited federal review

**Holding:** Section 1257(a) permits review under Cox Broadcasting's first finality category because the preclusion ruling conclusively bars Richards and Hill's remaining federal claims and leaves its implementation preordained, despite the state interlocutory label. Review concerns only nonparty due process; separate Buck Act proceedings and the removed tax-merits question do not enlarge it.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Stone-Zsela's unanimous Opinion of the Court. All nine participating Justices join Parts I–IV and every following holding and qualification; there is no separate writing.

**Material application:** State Counts 3 and 4 had already been rejected. The ruling disposes of these petitioners' remaining federal claims, while the remaining Buck Act claim belongs to a separate federal-employee group. The actual orders leave implementation preordained; state certification alone establishes neither federal finality nor a presumed later judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Reversal permits litigation to continue, so the fourth-category arbitration route used in Southland and Casarotto does not supply this jurisdiction. Quackenbush's §1291 routes concern federal appellate review, not this state judgment under §1257. Neither tax constitutionality, Buck Act liability nor entitlement to a particular tax remedy is decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Cox Broadcasting Corp. v. Cohn, 420 U.S. 469, supplies its first category for practically concluded claims and preordained implementation. Southland Corp. v. Keating, 465 U.S. 1, is distinguished as a fourth-category arbitration case. Doctor's Associates, Inc. v. Casarotto, May 20, 1996, preserves attention to actual proceedings rather than certification alone, but its arbitration finality and substantive FAA holdings do not decide this case. Quackenbush v. Allstate Insurance Co., June 3, 1996, retains its distinct §1291 and abstention holdings and the separate tax-comity setting; it supplies no reopening of the earlier federal tax dismissal.

##### Whether shared interests alone authorize binding absent claimants

**Holding:** A State's ordinary preclusion rules remain subject to due process: essentially identical interests, vigorous advocacy, resources and public importance alone do not authorize adjudication of absent persons' own legal rights. Binding may rest on constitutionally sufficient consent, control, genuine privity, adequate class or representative litigation, or another recognized relationship or special remedial arrangement consistent with due process; finality policy and a virtual-representation label cannot replace that prerequisite.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Stone-Zsela's unanimous Opinion of the Court.

**Material application:** Guardian–ward and trustee–beneficiary relationships illustrate genuine privity; bankruptcy and probate illustrate special arrangements that may lawfully foreclose successive litigation. Each person need not appear individually, but common objectives cannot alone confer representative authority. Section 1738 does not separately command Alabama to bind these strangers to its own earlier judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court assumes without deciding that adequate representation might cure absent notice in some class or representative proceedings. Even on that assumption, this representative basis fails. The assumption neither makes representation an automatic cure nor establishes a universal individual-notice requirement. Conversely, notice, objections or opt-out opportunities do not independently cure inadequate representation, and mere notice of another person's suit supplies neither representative authority nor a general intervention duty.

Ordinary state preclusion and valid finality remain. Correction of this extreme application supplies no general retrial of ordinary state-law errors and decides none of Matsushita's full collateral-review reservation. Absence of a Rule 23 class is relevant here but does not itself invalidate every source-authorized representative judgment; Bedingfield remains effective among persons properly bound.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Hansberry v. Lee, 311 U.S. 32, supplies the constitutional requirement of genuine adequate representation while preserving lawful representative adjudication. Martin v. Wilks, 490 U.S. 755, supplies nonparty hearing protection with adequate-representation, control and due-process-consistent special-remedial exceptions; notice alone creates no general intervention duty. Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, February 27, 1996, distinguishes ordinary recognition from constitutional authority to bind, including representation independent of notice and opt-out protections; its complete collateral-review reservation remains unresolved.

##### Whether the stated public-interest rationale binds these direct personal tax claims

**Holding:** The representative rationale used below does not constitutionally bind Richards and Hill to Bedingfield's disposition of their own tax liability and claims for paid sums. Shared taxpayer objectives and fiscal reliance do not establish the required relationship; genuine representative public-right litigation and ordinary reliance on precedent remain distinct and preserved.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Stone-Zsela's unanimous Opinion of the Court.

**Material application:** Richards and Hill were nonparties to the nonclass Bedingfield action. The earlier litigants' common constitutional objective, resources and vigorous advocacy, together with revenue and bond-financing reliance, do not establish authority over these absent taxpayers' personal claims. No deficient advocacy finding is necessary. Petitioners' no-notice assertion is not an appellate finding; no participation or control finding supplies the rationale used below, and every possible connection need not be resolved or found absent to reject it.
