Churchill, May 31, 1994, retains its distinct particular-speech and reasonable-account rules. Simon & Schuster v. Members of the New York State Crime Victims Board, December 10, 1991, supplies the relevance of financial burdens. Turner Broadcasting System v. FCC, June 27, 1994, preserves substantial but reviewable legislative predictive judgments; it does not decide this employment case.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 501(b) prohibits receipt of an honorarium by a Member, officer or employee; §505(2) excludes special Government employees as defined in 18 U.S.C. §202. Section 505(3) covers money or anything of value for an appearance, speech or article; a series falls within the definition if its subject directly relates to official duties or payment is because of governmental status. That parenthesis does not already restrict every single honorarium. Actual and necessary travel expenses for the individual and one relative are excluded to the extent another person pays or reimburses them, and otherwise deducted to the extent they are not paid or reimbursed. Travel expenses mean transportation, lodging and meals away from residence or principal employment.

Section 501(c) treats a payment on the individual's behalf to a §170(c) charitable organization as not received, subject to a $2,000 ceiling on each payment and no financial benefit to the individual, parent, sibling, spouse, child or dependent relative. Section 504(a) permits an Attorney General civil action and a civil penalty up to the greater of $10,000 or compensation received. Under §504(b), a written opinion from an entity identified by §503 protects its recipient and another covered person whose facts are materially indistinguishable from subsection (a)'s sanction only for conduct after issuance, in good faith and in accordance with the opinion's provisions and findings. These are qualified provisions, not general exemptions. The separate §501(a) high-level outside-income ceiling is not adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part and remanded. Nonenforcement of the existing ban throughout the certified below-GS–16 class is preserved by five votes; Crane retains individual relief by six; relief for unrepresented senior employees is removed. Congressional and judicial coverage, §501(a)'s outside-income ceiling and valid independent ethics restrictions are not invalidated. No penalty or compensation award is entered.

## Source: [state/holdings/first-amendment.md](../../../state/holdings/first-amendment.md)

#### Board of County Commissioners, Wabaunsee County v. Umbehr, 518 U.S. 668 (1996)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-1654.
**Decided:** June 28, 1996.

##### Whether an existing independent contractor can challenge speech-retaliatory termination

**Holding:** Government may not terminate or refuse to renew an existing independent-contractor relationship because the contractor spoke as a private citizen on a matter of public concern when the speech and public interests outweigh the government's legitimate contracting and operational interests. Courts apply Pickering's balance to the actual contractual relationship, with appropriate regard for genuine service needs; contractor status, retained termination power and absence of a property entitlement do not by themselves defeat the claim.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices.

**Material application:** The County’s existing renewable exclusive arrangement carried valuable benefits despite no direct purchase of hauling. Loss of exclusivity, coordinated negotiation and notice protection can cause injury notwithstanding later direct city contracts. Content, form and context determine public concern. Actual independence, other clients, contacts and responsibilities may affect the balance either way; private communication is not automatically unprotected and offense at criticism is not a service interest.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No initial-bidder, universal future-award, pure affiliation, unrelated employee-status, comprehensive factual-mistake, later-acquired-evidence or mitigation holding. Summary-judgment assumptions establish no actual speech protection, motive, injury or ultimate balance.

##### What causation and same-decision requirements govern the remand

**Holding:** The contractor must establish that protected speech was a substantial or motivating factor in the challenged action. The government may defeat the retaliation claim by proving by a preponderance of the evidence that it would have taken the same action absent the protected speech; identifying a merely possible lawful reason does not establish that defense. Official-capacity liability and relief also require the ordinary entity-attribution and remedial predicates.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices.

**Material application:** The claimant’s motivating-factor burden and government’s preponderance counterfactual defense remain separate. Genuine performance, efficiency, cost, trust and operational grounds remain available; their possible existence is not proof. Personal immunity is distinct from official-capacity entity liability.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Waters/Settle do not supply a universal investigation framework. Ordinary Monell attribution and remedy predicates remain necessary.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reviewed official-capacity judgment affirmed and remanded, 7–2. The individual-immunity judgment and McClure’s separate causation judgment are undisturbed; no automatic renewal, reinstatement, damages or trial.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Pickering and Connick:** Extended to existing-contractor retaliation through a balance adjusted to the actual service relationship, preserving private-citizen/public-concern and genuine operational-interest inquiries.
- **Perry v. Sindermann:** Applied to distinguish absence of entitlement to continued benefits from an unconstitutional retaliatory condition.
- **Givhan:** Preserves protection for qualifying citizen expression communicated privately; delivery method alone does not resolve the claim.
- **Mt. Healthy:** Applied without changing the motivating-factor burden or the government's preponderance same-decision defense.
- **Waters and Settle:** Their actual genuine-ground and causation distinctions remain operative; no full investigation framework or unrelated curricular rule is adopted here.
- **National Treasury Employees Union:** Its broad prospective-restraint rule remains distinct from this individual termination; its full justification allocation is not automatically imported.
- **Kentucky v. Graham and Monell:** Preserve the distinction between personal immunity and entity claims and the independent requirements for governmental attribution and relief.

## Source: [state/holdings/first-amendment.md](../../../state/holdings/first-amendment.md)

#### Thomasson v. Perry

**Docket or dockets:** Fourth Circuit No. 95-2185; review of 80 F.3d 915 (en banc).

**Decided:** 1997-06-09.

For the equal-protection question and statutory qualifications in Thomasson v. Perry, Fourth Circuit No. 95-2185, decided June 9, 1997, see Civil Rights — Military personnel and equal protection.

##### Relevant evidentiary use of this voluntary statement does not violate the First Amendment

**Holding:** The First Amendment does not prohibit using Thomasson's voluntary identity statement as relevant evidence of the lawful conduct-related personnel predicate sustained in Part I, with the genuine rebuttal opportunity present here. That evidentiary use does not authorize discharge merely for opposing the policy, punish abstract belief, or make every identity statement an admission of a prohibited act.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Breyer; five direct joins.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The relevant connection rests on the lawful conduct-related inference and meaningful rebuttal sustained in Part I. A bare evidentiary label cannot justify punishment of expression. Advocacy retaliation, an irrelevant inference and abstract belief remain outside the holding. Neither a completed prohibited act nor realized operational disruption is required for this bounded predictive use.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reject the independent speech challenge by five direct joins. Stevens would independently reverse on speech; Stone-Zsela, Souter and Ginsburg reserve that question and supply no additional support for rejecting it.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Dawson forbids punishment of irrelevant abstract beliefs; Mitchell permits relevant expression as evidence without importing its criminal predicates into every personnel case. Waters preserves scrutiny of the ground actually relied on without adding a universal investigation checklist or actual-disruption condition.

## Source: [state/holdings/property-and-economic-rights.md](../../../state/holdings/property-and-economic-rights.md)

#### United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1180.

**Decided:** 1993-12-13.

##### Notice and a hearing before civil seizure of real property

**Holding:** Before seizing real property for civil forfeiture, the Government must ordinarily give the owner notice and a meaningful opportunity to be heard; a Fourth Amendment warrant does not itself satisfy this independent Fifth Amendment requirement. To bypass prior process for exigent circumstances, the Government must show that less restrictive measures, such as notice of the pending claim recorded against the property, a restraining order, or a bond, would not prevent sale, destruction, or continued unlawful use; emergency seizure remains possible on that showing with appropriate subsequent process.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion for the Court, Pre-seizure process portion, joined by Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg. These six adopt the same rule, its exigency qualification, and its application.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Soldal v. Cook County: applied to preserve the independent operation of constitutional protections; its possessory-seizure rule remains intact.
- Mathews v. Eldridge: applied to the process required before this deprivation; no new general balancing formula replaces it.
- Connecticut v. Doehr: applied for the value of notice and an opportunity to contest a prejudgment property deprivation.
- Fuentes v. Shevin: applied for the value of notice and an opportunity to contest a prejudgment property deprivation.
- Calero-Toledo v. Pearson Yacht Leasing Co.: distinguished because movable property and exceptional governmental needs can justify delayed process; that exception remains available where its justification exists.

##### Mandatory administrative promptness does not supply an unstated dismissal sanction

**Holding:** Noncompliance with the distinct reporting and commencement duties in 19 U.S.C. §§1602–1604 does not require dismissal of a forfeiture action filed within §1621's five-year limitations period. Section 1602 requires immediate reporting by customs agents to customs officers; §1603(b) requires prompt reporting to the United States attorney when the specified seizure or discovery requires proceedings by that office; and §1604, for recovery requiring district-court or Court of International Trade proceedings, requires those proceedings to be commenced forthwith when the prescribed probable basis for a fine, penalty, or forfeiture appears, subject to the Attorney General's preserved determination, after inquiry, that such proceedings probably cannot be sustained or that public justice does not require institution or prosecution, in which event the facts are instead reported to the Treasury Secretary for direction. These remain mandatory duties within their stated conditions, and the ruling does not extinguish an independently supported constitutional-delay claim or other source-authorized relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion for the Court, Statutory timing portion, joined by Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg. All nine adopt this construction and reversal of the contrary ground below.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Brock v. Pierce County: applied to distinguish a mandatory timing duty from an unstated loss of governmental authority.
- United States v. Montalvo-Murillo: applied to reject an automatic substantive remedy that the timing provision does not prescribe; its distinct hearing duty is not diluted.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The notice holding concerns civil real-property forfeiture, not criminal forfeiture or ultimate forfeiture liability. Statutory promptness remains mandatory; an independently supported constitutional-delay claim or otherwise authorized relief is not extinguished.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The hearing ruling is affirmed, 6–3, and the statutory-dismissal ground reversed, 9–0; the case is remanded. The timely forfeiture action may proceed lawfully. Restoration, accounting or other consequences of the interim seizure require an independently authorized basis; no award, automatic dismissal, return of title or sovereign damages is ordered.

## Selected current standards and Court setting

## Standards and Tests selection: Commerce connection, mechanism, and class-wide effects

### Commerce connection, mechanism, and class-wide effects

**Governing question:** How courts determine whether a regulated class falls within Congress’s Commerce Clause authority, including when aggregation, a case-specific nexus, or inclusion within a broader federal regime may perform constitutional work.

**Current rule:** The Court first identifies the class Congress actually regulated and the interstate mechanism asserted to connect that class to commerce among the several States. Interstate commerce itself, channels, instrumentalities/persons/things genuinely in commerce, and properly defined economic classes participating in or materially affecting actual interstate markets remain established routes. Congress may also include intrastate conduct when inclusion is reasonably necessary to prevent evasion or material impairment of a valid federal interstate regulatory regime. Aggregate magnitude and substantiality may measure the effect of a class only after a constitutionally sufficient connection has been identified; generalized chains through crime, education, health, insurance, consumption, productivity, public expenditure, or similar downstream consequences cannot themselves create the missing connection.

The court independently determines whether the asserted interstate mechanism is constitutionally eligible. Once that mechanism exists, Congress receives substantial latitude on predictive judgment, magnitude, and practical regulatory design. A reasonable and judicially identifiable fit must exist between the class Congress enacted and the mechanism supporting authority. The limiting-principle inquiry tests whether the asserted mechanism leaves an enforceable distinction between the enumerated commerce power and a general police power; it is not a free-form balance of federal and State interests.

A court may not save an unambiguous federal offense by inserting an omitted commerce element. A statutory jurisdictional element is relevant only insofar as the connection it requires actually performs constitutional work; the mere existence of jurisdictional words is not itself a safe harbor. As of this date the Court has not adopted a general rule governing remote historical movement in every statute.

**Authority by component:**

- Architecture; connection before magnitude; recognition of necessary inclusion; no-insertion rule: United States v. Lopez, April 26, 1995; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela’s Opinion of the Court joined by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.
- Economic vehicle-taking class and enacted vehicle nexus: United States v. Harris, April 27, 1995; O’Connor’s eight-Justice commerce rationale. Stone concurs in the judgment on the narrower direct-instrumentality/vehicle-specific nexus ground.
- Direct interference with an interstate service market: American Life League v. Reno, November 13, 1995; Kennedy Part I, unanimous.
- Application of Harris without new doctrine: United States v. Bishop; United States v. Stokes, February 5, 1996; O’Connor Part II, unanimous.
- Anti-evasion/material-impairment rule for the post-1986 machinegun regime: United States v. Hale, February 12, 1996; O’Connor Part I, unanimous.
- Interstate commercial transmission as commerce itself: Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, April 28, 1997; unanimous commerce holding.
- Legally fixed cross-border child-support obligation: United States v. Mussari; United States v. Schroeder, June 30, 1997; O’Connor, unanimous.

**Present operation effective:** Mussari/Schroeder, June 30, 1997, for the latest application; the controlling architecture dates to Lopez, April 26, 1995, and the anti-evasion/material-impairment branch dates to Hale, February 12, 1996.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:**

1. Define the enacted regulated class.
2. Identify the asserted interstate-commerce connection.
3. Identify the mechanism connecting the class to interstate commerce.
4. Determine whether the mechanism is constitutionally eligible.
5. Only then use aggregation or substantiality to evaluate class-wide magnitude.
6. Give Congress substantial latitude on prediction and regulatory design within the valid power.
7. Test whether the statutory class reasonably fits the asserted mechanism.
8. Confirm that the theory leaves an enforceable constitutional boundary.

**Exceptions and limits:** Noneconomic intrastate conduct is not categorically immune. Traditional State subject matter is not categorically immune. Formal legislative findings are neither universally required nor sufficient by themselves. Wickard and Perez retain their economic-class scope. Garcia’s rejection of traditional-governmental-function immunity remains intact. Remote movement and generalized social costs independently establish none of the current Commerce grounds. The Court has not yet adopted a general material-interstate-externality doctrine. Mussari/Schroeder directly regulates the private obligor: it commands no State administration and gives no federal power to issue or modify support, custody, parentage or domestic-status orders. The cross-border payment rule adds no flight, travel, voluntary-crossing, bank-wire or evasion-motive element. Not every protester is an interstate enterprise; the service-market ground reaches conduct directed at provision or receipt of the covered services. Hale preserves § 922(o)'s government-related channels and prior-lawful pre-ban stock. No Second Amendment, amended-statute or independent indictment rule is established by these commerce holdings.

**Material development:**

- April 26, 1995 — Lopez: ADDED / CHANGED. Added the controlling decision sequence of regulated class → interstate mechanism → magnitude/aggregation → fit/boundary. Changed the prior substantial-effects law only to the extent necessary to reject aggregation of generalized downstream consequences as a substitute for the missing Commerce connection. Preserved Wickard and Perez within their genuine economic-class settings. Reserved fuller Necessary-and-Proper inclusion questions.
- April 27, 1995 — Harris: CLARIFIED / APPLIED. Clarified that Lopez does not disable direct regulation of an economic vehicle-taking class connected to an interstate motor-vehicle regime when the statute separately requires a particular-vehicle interstate nexus. Stone’s narrower concurrence emphasizes direct protection of the transportation instrumentality. No general remote-movement rule is added.
- November 13, 1995 — American Life League: CLARIFIED / APPLIED. Clarified that intentional obstruction directed at provision or receipt of services can be regulated when it directly interferes with an actual interstate service market. This is not downstream crime-cost aggregation.
- February 5, 1996 — Bishop/Stokes: APPLIED ONLY. Applied Harris. No new Commerce rule.
- February 12, 1996 — Hale: ADDED. Added the first operational anti-evasion/material-impairment doctrine. Intrastate possession may be included where excluding it would materially undermine or permit evasion of a valid federal interstate market regime. This development uses the Necessary and Proper Clause together with Commerce authority. Hale does not create a general firearm possession power.
- April 28, 1997 — Pic-A-State: CLARIFIED / APPLIED. Clarified that a knowing interstate computer transmission operating as part of a commercial procurement transaction is interstate commerce itself, making aggregate-effects analysis unnecessary.
- June 30, 1997 — Mussari/Schroeder: CLARIFIED / APPLIED. Clarified that Congress may protect a legally fixed payment obligation whose statutory relationship is itself cross-border. The different-State relation performs constitutional work; family-policy importance and social costs do not.

**Holdings navigation:** United States v. Lopez; United States v. Harris; American Life League v. Reno; United States v. Bishop; United States v. Stokes, judgment below 66 F.3d 569; United States v. Hale, judgment below 978 F.2d 1016; Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno; United States v. Mussari; United States v. Schroeder.

## Standards and Tests selection: State-created liberty in prison discipline

### State-created liberty in prison discipline

**Governing question:** When substantive prison law creates a protected liberty interest.

**Current rule:** A State creates protected prison liberty when substantive law meaningfully restricts officials’ authority to impose a significant deprivation. Mandatory words alone are insufficient: courts examine the genuine constraint in the whole disciplinary scheme and whether the restraint is meaningful rather than routine. This is a sufficient, nonexclusive state-entitlement rule; state codification is unnecessary for directly protected constitutional liberty.

**Authority by component:**

- Substantive state constraints and meaningful prison liberty: Substantive state constraints and meaningful prison liberty, 1995-06-19. Breyer’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; five Justices adopt this sufficient, nonexclusive state-entitlement rule.

**Present operation effective:** 1995-06-19; the component authorities identified above.

**Exceptions and limits:** Other direct constitutional, unexpected-restraint and sentence-duration protections are not displaced. A protected interest and required process need their own determinations. No new multifactor test, categorical rejection of punitive constraints or automatic liability follows.

**Holdings navigation:** Substantive state constraints and meaningful prison liberty.

## Standards and Tests selection: Revocation of existing parole-like community liberty

### Revocation of existing parole-like community liberty

**Governing question:** Termination of a prisoner's existing release to ordinary community life under parole-like conditions.

**Current rule:** When a State releases a prisoner to live at home, work and conduct ordinary community life under parole-like supervision, ending that existing conditional liberty requires Morrissey's preliminary and final protections. Retained legal custody, an overcrowding purpose, the preparole label or a separate formal-parole decision does not eliminate the interest. A constitutionally adequate determination must precede final revocation; a completed final hearing need not invariably precede initial arrest or detention.

**Authority by component:**

- Young v. Harper — Part II — Existing parole-like community liberty requires protection, 1997-03-18; Part II joined by all nine supplies the actual-liberty trigger
- Young v. Harper — Part III — Morrissey’s ordinary stages and qualifications govern, 1997-03-18; Part III joined by all nine supplies the process and its distinct exceptions

**Present operation effective:** Young v. Harper, 1997-03-18.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** When the participant is arrested and detained pending revocation, a reasonably prompt inquiry at or reasonably near the place of the alleged violation or arrest determines probable cause or reasonable ground for detention for a release-condition violation. A person not directly involved in the case decides; that person need not be a judge and may be a different parole officer. The participant receives notice of the inquiry, its purpose and the alleged violations, may appear and speak, and may present relevant documents and witnesses. On request he may question an adverse informant in his presence. If the officer determines that identifying the informant would expose that person to harm, confrontation and cross-examination of that informant may be withheld. The officer summarizes the proceedings and states the reasons and evidence supporting the determination; formal judicial findings are unnecessary. Supported probable cause permits continued detention and institutional return pending final decision.

The State must offer a final revocation hearing, if desired, within a reasonable time after custody and before effective final revocation. It must resolve contested relevant facts and whether the circumstances warrant revocation, allowing denial and mitigation. Minimum protections are written notice of the claimed violations or asserted grounds; disclosure of adverse evidence; an opportunity to be heard personally and present witnesses and documents; confrontation and cross-examination unless the officer specifically finds good cause to withhold them; a neutral and detached decisionmaker, whose members need not be judges or lawyers; and a written statement of the evidence relied on and reasons.

Informal evidence, including letters and affidavits, may be considered. Issues already determined adversely in another forum, including a relevant criminal conviction, need not be relitigated. Morrissey’s discussion of two months establishes no universal deadline or safe harbor. Preliminary informant risk and final-stage specific good cause are distinct exceptions, each permitting rather than requiring withholding upon the necessary finding. The separate counsel question remains undecided.

**Exceptions and limits:** Initial parole eligibility, complete state-program entitlement and independently lawful custody grounds remain distinct. The decision supplies no new evidentiary burden or automatic-release consequence.

**Holdings navigation:** Young v. Harper (1997-03-18) — Criminal Procedure / Conditional community release and revocation; Young v. Harper (1997-03-18) — Which preliminary and final safeguards govern withdrawal of that liberty.

## Standards and Tests selection: Effective prisoner court access and demonstrated obstruction

### Effective prisoner court access and demonstrated obstruction

**Governing question:** The substance and remedial scope of a Bounds access claim.

**Current rule:** A prison must provide a meaningful practical opportunity for court access through effective libraries, trained assistance or other effective arrangements assessed as a whole; books alone do not suffice for prisoners unable to use them without needed help. Access liability requires actual or evidence-supported imminent hindrance of a qualifying nonfrivolous legal claim, subject to restrictions reasonably related to legitimate penological interests. The prisoner need not win the obstructed case or complete an impossible filing; an abstract resource deficiency or preferred staffing plan is insufficient. Direct and collateral sentence challenges and prison-conditions claims qualify, and genuine state-created-liberty claims remain possible under Sandin's actual rule.

Common relief requires a demonstrated common barrier and identified affected group, with supported geographic and functional reach; every member need not already have lost a lawsuit. Relief must fit the proved wrong, respect valid security restrictions and give the State a genuine opportunity to design an effective cure. Necessary implementation may be ordered without treating each program measure as an independent constitutional entitlement.

**Authority by component:**

- Effective arrangements: Lewis v. Casey — Holding I, June 24, 1996; Kennedy Part I for all nine.
- Substantive nonfrivolous-claim hindrance: Holding II, Kennedy for Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas and himself (five).
- Supported common relief, remedial fit, security and State design: Holding III, Kennedy for all except Stevens (eight).

**Present operation effective:** Lewis v. Casey, June 24, 1996.

**Exceptions and limits:** There is no entitlement to a preferred library, physical browsing in every case, counsel for every prisoner or numerical program levels. Ordinary personal injury, causation and stage-appropriate proof remain; the additional preliminary Article III merits screen has only four votes. No categorical exclusion of every other important retained right or cessation of every assistance duty upon filing is adopted; independent communications and petition claims retain their own predicates. A valid security restriction does not become unconstitutional merely because delay causes an access injury, but an unsupported security assertion proves no validity.

**Related current rules:** Prison-conditions prospective relief applies the original §3626 requirements to any lawful remedy.

**Holdings navigation:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343 (1996).

## Standards and Tests selection: Circumstantial proof of unconstitutional motive despite qualified immunity

### Circumstantial proof of unconstitutional motive despite qualified immunity

**Governing question:** Proof, discovery and causation when unconstitutional motive is an element of a claim and qualified immunity is asserted.

**Current rule:** Qualified immunity imposes no categorical direct-evidence requirement for unconstitutional motive. The plaintiff must establish the actual constitutional elements, personal responsibility and causation through legally competent direct or circumstantial evidence under ordinary procedural burdens. Specific nonconclusory support, lawful discovery limits and the clearly-established-law defense remain available; the rule abolishes neither all heightened specificity requirements nor a burden supplied by the underlying claim. Ordinary preponderance governs unconstitutional motive in the §1983 retaliation claim; immunity and litigation burden cannot justify a special clear-and-convincing quantum. Courts may decide dispositive legal defenses first and require specific nonconclusory support, but cannot categorically withhold discovery needed to obtain proof until the rejected heightened showing is made. Rule-authorized clarification, replies, sequencing, protective orders, relevant proportional discovery and ordinary summary-judgment duties remain available. Under Mt. Healthy the claimant must establish protected conduct and its substantial or motivating causal role; the defendant may then prove by a preponderance that the same action would have occurred without it. A merely possible lawful explanation does not establish that defense or exclude competent motive evidence.

**Authority by component:**

- Circumstantial proof remains available in motive-dependent claims: Circumstantial proof remains available in motive-dependent claims, 1995-06-12. Ginsburg's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer; nine votes support the rule and its operative limits.

- Crawford-El v. Britton — Proof of unconstitutional motive, 1997-06-16; Stone-Zsela, all nine
- Crawford-El v. Britton — Discovery and stage-specific screening, 1997-06-16; all nine
- Crawford-El v. Britton — Actual causation and independent defenses, 1997-06-16; all nine

**Present operation effective:** Crawford-El, 1997-06-16, rejecting a special proof quantum and derivative categorical discovery barrier while preserving the carried direct/circumstantial-evidence rule.

**Holdings navigation:** Circumstantial proof remains available in motive-dependent claims. Crawford-El v. Britton (1997-06-16) — Civil Rights / Qualified immunity and motive evidence.

## Standards and Tests selection: Political retaliation in existing government service relationships

### Political retaliation in existing government service relationships

**Governing question:** Termination, nonrenewal or withdrawal of an existing independent-contractor or recurring service-provider relationship for citizen speech or partisan allegiance.

**Current rule:** Government may not terminate or refuse to renew an existing contractor relationship because of private-citizen speech on a matter of public concern when the speaker's and public's interests outweigh legitimate contracting and operational interests. Apply Connick's content, form and context inquiry and Pickering's balance adjusted to the actual service relationship, including genuine performance, efficiency, cost, trust and disruption concerns; independence, other clients, contacts and responsibilities may matter in either direction. Neither contractor status, termination authority, lack of property entitlement nor absence of direct governmental purchase categorically defeats protection. Private delivery alone does not remove qualifying expression.

For withdrawal based on refusing partisan support or supporting an opponent, apply the distinct Elrod–Branti rule: government must demonstrate that political affiliation is appropriate for effective performance of the actual work. Labels, general patronage advantages and dislike of opposition do not suffice; genuinely policymaking or confidential functions may justify the qualification. Public-concern speech and materially mixed speech/affiliation claims require their appropriate service-adjusted inquiry rather than an undifferentiated affiliation or management rule.

The claimant must show protected conduct was a substantial or motivating factor. Government may defeat the claim by proving by a preponderance that the same decision would have occurred absent that conduct; a merely possible lawful reason does not establish the defense. Entity attribution, personal defenses, injury and relief retain their separate requirements.

**Authority by component:**

- Existing-contractor expression and the causation sequence: Protected expression in an existing contractual relationship and Causation, the same-decision defense and the limited remand, 1996-06-28; Souter, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer (seven).
- Recurring-provider affiliation, mixed claims and causation: Political affiliation in an existing recurring service relationship, Distinguishing affiliation demands from speech disputes and Causation, defenses and relief, 1996-06-28; Stevens, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer (seven).

**Present operation effective:** Umbehr and O’Hare, 1996-06-28; uncoordinated same-day decisions combined prospectively.

**Open question:** Initial unsuccessful bidders, every future award and general employee status for unrelated purposes are not decided. No comprehensive factual-error or investigation framework is adopted.

**Related current rules:** “Public-employee speech and the employer’s mistaken factual account” retains its limited genuine-ground rules; “Prospective restrictions on employees’ citizen expression” governs its distinct broad advance restraint.

**Holdings navigation:** Board of County Commissioners, Wabaunsee County v. Umbehr, 518 U.S. 668 (1996); O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake, 518 U.S. 712 (1996).

## Standards and Tests selection: Backward-looking relief for unconstitutional taxes

### Backward-looking relief for unconstitutional taxes

**Governing question:** What instructions govern backward-looking relief for unconstitutional taxes?

**Current rule:** Applying a federal tax rule retroactively does not alone determine remedy. When the State required payment before adequate opportunity to contest, due process requires meaningful backward-looking relief; lawful refunds, retroactive nondiscriminatory assessments or a combination may suffice. Meaningful prepayment review presents a different remedial setting. State law and due process govern the correction; no automatic refund of every payment follows. Even if an adequate prepayment route existed, a State that held out a clear postpayment remedy for illegally assessed and collected taxes, including voluntary payments, may not withdraw that remedy after payment and rely on failure to use the other route. It must afford a clear and certain opportunity for a refund or constitutionally adequate equivalent relief, subject to ordinary proof of payment and amount. A prospective adequate prepayment system remains possible with fair notice.

**Authority by component:**

- Retroactivity and the lawful tax remedy are separate: Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation — Retroactivity and the lawful tax remedy are separate, 1993-06-18. Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Retroactivity and the lawful tax remedy are separate portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

- A State may not withdraw its apparent postpayment remedy after payment: A State may not withdraw its apparent postpayment remedy after payment, 1994-12-06. Justice O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer: nine direct votes for the bounded procedural rule and remedy.

**Present operation effective:** Reich v. Collins, December 6, 1994, for the after-payment withdrawal limit; Harper’s distinct remedial-choice rules remain.

**Holdings navigation:** Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993); A State may not withdraw its apparent postpayment remedy after payment.

## Standards and Tests selection: Factual frivolousness and review of fee-screening dismissals

### Factual frivolousness and review of fee-screening dismissals

**Governing question:** Dismissal as factually frivolous under then-effective 28 U.S.C. §1915(d), and appellate review of that screening determination.

**Current rule:** Under former §1915(d), where that provision governs, a court may dismiss a claim proceeding without prepayment when its factual allegations are clearly baseless—irrational or wholly incredible—even without judicially noticeable contrary facts. The initial assessment must favor the plaintiff; mere improbability, extraordinary circumstances or ordinary disbelief is insufficient, and screening cannot decide genuine factual disputes. Review is for abuse of discretion, with legal errors independently correctable. The reviewing court must address relevant circumstances, including pro se status, resolution of genuine factual disputes, legal errors, adequacy of reasons, prejudice and whether more specific pleading could cure the defect. Such dismissal is not a merits adjudication barring a paid complaint with the same allegations; its possible effect on later fee-screening decisions makes prejudice and curability material.

**Authority by component:**
- Irrational-or-wholly-incredible threshold and plaintiff-favorable limit: Denton v. Hernandez, May 4, 1992, “Factual frivolousness under section 1915(d).”
- Review, relevant circumstances and nonmerits character: the same record, “Appellate review and the consequences of dismissal.” O'Connor for Stone, White, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas controls both propositions; the dissenters' agreement with the factual standard is not a whole-opinion join.

**Present operation effective:** Denton, May 4, 1992, within its former-law scope; PLRA, April 26, 1996, replaces the statutory provision.

**Exceptions and limits:** Corroborating affidavits are not a pleading prerequisite. Rule 12(b)(6) merits pleading remains distinct from factual-frivolity screening.

**Open question:** No absolute notice-and-amendment entitlement or comprehensive preclusion rule for later fee applications is adopted.

**Holdings navigation:** Denton v. Hernandez, 504 U.S. 25 (1992) — Fee-waiver screening and appellate review.

**Effective transition:** PLRA §§802–804 — Source, authority and operative text, §804(a)(5), April 26, 1996, replaces the former screening provision with mandatory §1915(e)(2) grounds and separate counsel discretion. See Dismissal and counsel requests under amended §1915. Denton's former-law rule is retained for its surviving temporal and interpretive relevance; no current-term holding settles every new statutory ground, review standard or disputed pending-case application.

## Standards and Tests selection: Statutory temporal reach and new consequences for completed conduct

### Statutory temporal reach and new consequences for completed conduct

**Governing question:** How courts decide whether new legislation governs events preceding enactment.

**Current rule:** First determine whether Congress prescribed the statute’s temporal reach. If it did, that direction governs subject to independent constitutional limits. Otherwise ask whether applying the provision would attach new legal consequences to completed events by impairing existing rights, increasing liability for past conduct or imposing new duties concerning completed transactions. Such operation requires clear congressional intent. Pending litigation alone does not answer the question. Procedural, jurisdictional and prospective-relief labels are not substitutes for examining the provision’s actual operation. Legislative correction of a judicial construction, or a purpose to restore an earlier understanding, does not by itself establish retrospective reach; this legislative inquiry differs from the temporal operation of judicial decisions.

**Authority by component:**

- Sequential temporal inquiry: Landgraf v. USI Film Products — Determine statutory temporal reach before applying the presumption against new retrospective consequences, 1994-04-26. Stevens’s general-framework portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg; five Justices adopt this organizing rule. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur on a distinct relevant-activity approach, not as additional joins at this level of generality.
- Corrective purpose and distinction from judicial interpretation: Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc — Corrective legislative purpose does not itself give an amendment retrospective reach, 1994-04-26. Stevens’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, controls by five votes at this explanatory level. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas accept the judicial/legislative distinction through their separate textual method, not through a presumed full join.

**Present operation effective:** Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc, 1994-04-26.

**Holdings navigation:** Determine statutory temporal reach before applying the presumption against new retrospective consequences; Corrective legislative purpose does not itself give an amendment retrospective reach.

**Exceptions and limits:** An express congressional direction concerning the temporal operation of a statute resolves the threshold statutory question, subject to applicable constitutional limits. Such a direction does not alone determine whether Congress may constitutionally require reconsideration of an already completed Article III judgment. Landgraf concerns statutory consequences for earlier conduct in litigation properly before the courts and does not decide that separate final-judgment issue.

**Related current rules:** Congressional reopening of completed private federal judgments supplies Morgan Stanley's and Plaut's separate Article III rule for §27A(b).

## Standards and Tests selection: Medicare corrective adjustments and permissible interpretive change

### Medicare corrective adjustments and permissible interpretive change

**Governing question:** What instructions govern medicare corrective adjustments and permissible interpretive change?

**Current rule:** Section 1395x(v)(1)(A)(ii) commands suitable retroactive corrective adjustments when aggregate reimbursement for a provider and period proves inadequate or excessive. The Secretary may construe that command as reconciling interim payments to sums payable under lawful reasonable-cost methods and ceilings, without an individualized exception whenever actual costs exceed the ceiling. Authorized exceptions and independent challenges to unlawful methods remain. Interpretive change can reduce weight without automatically invalidating a considered permissible construction; text, delegation and reasons must be examined. Unsupported litigation positions neither replace required agency explanation nor gain automatic controlling weight. A changed interpretation is not disqualified merely by statutory age or a litigation setting, but unexplained reversal or disregard of legitimate reliance may be arbitrary and capricious. For antecedent transactions lacking clear prior agency guidance, a court may use the agency’s current authoritative statutory interpretation without backdating the regulation’s effective date.

**Authority by component:**

- Corrective adjustments operate within the authorized cost-method system: Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala — Corrective adjustments operate within the authorized cost-method system, 1993-06-07. White’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.
- A changed agency interpretation is not automatically disqualified: Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala — A changed agency interpretation is not automatically disqualified, 1993-06-07. White’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.
- Smiley — Part III-A, permissible explained change, 1996-06-03; all eight Associates; Stone-Zsela does not join.
- Smiley — Part V, qualified antecedent interpretive use, 1996-06-03; all eight Associates; the absence of clear contrary guidance is independently established in Part III-B.

**Present operation effective:** Smiley, June 3, 1996, for qualified antecedent interpretive use; Good Samaritan’s Medicare rule remains unchanged.

**Holdings navigation:** Good Samaritan Hospital v. Shalala, 508 U.S. 402 (1993); Smiley v. Citibank (South Dakota), N.A., 517 U.S. 735.

**Open question:** A later rule displacing clear earlier agency interpretation presents a materially different antecedent-conduct problem left unresolved. No universal authorization of retroactive legislative rulemaking, finding of actual reliance, or reopening of settled judgments follows.

## Standards and Tests selection: Medicare educational costs shifted from a related institution

### Medicare educational costs shifted from a related institution

**Governing question:** How the Medicare anti-redistribution regulation limits otherwise eligible education costs.

**Current rule:** The Secretary may reasonably apply §413.85(c)’s prohibition on redistribution of costs from educational institutions to hospitals to deny newly shifted costs formerly borne by a related college. Eligibility as an educational activity does not create an unconditional reimbursement right. Section 413.17’s cost-related-organization rule does not override the separate redistribution limitation. The validity of the actual agency ground must be assessed under the regulation and the relevant agency practice; neither an invented historical-cost ceiling nor a different rationale can replace that ground.

**Authority by component:**

- Anti-redistribution limit and review of the actual agency rationale: Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala — The redistribution provision reasonably limits newly shifted educational costs; The actual agency ground survives the asserted inconsistency, 1994-06-24. Justice Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, Part I, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Blackmun, Scalia and Souter. Five participating Justices directly adopt this regulatory construction, its application and its limits. Justice Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, Part II, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Blackmun, Scalia and Souter. The same five Justices adopt this answer to the consistency and actual-ground objections; it supports application of the redistribution holding rather than furnishing an alternative reimbursement bar.

**Present operation effective:** Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala, 1994-06-24.

**Related current rules:** The existing rules for interpretation of an agency’s own regulations and corrective Medicare adjustments remain effective; this is a particular reimbursement limitation, not a new general deference test.

**Holdings navigation:** The redistribution provision reasonably limits newly shifted educational costs; The actual agency ground survives the asserted inconsistency.

## Standards and Tests selection: Medicare accounting records and lawful allocation explanations

### Medicare accounting records and lawful allocation explanations

**Governing question:** Whether GAAP recognition and an interpretive manual compel or defeat a reimbursement timing rule.

**Current rule:** Section 1395x(v)(1)(A) and §§413.20 and 413.24 make adequate, verifiable accrual records the accounting base but do not make every GAAP recognition rule conclusive for Medicare reimbursement. Lawful reasonable-cost methods may distribute relevant advance-refunding expense elements across benefited periods. A manual explaining a permissible application of existing regulations is interpretive under APA §553(b)(A), rather than a new conflicting obligation requiring notice and comment. Neither interpretive form nor adjudication permits contradiction of binding law.

**Authority by component:**

- The accounting regulations do not compel immediate recognition of this loss: The accounting regulations do not compel immediate recognition of this loss, 1995-03-06. Kennedy’s opinion of the Court, Parts I–III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Five Justices expressly adopt this specific construction; no aggregation of different rationales or Marks rule is used.
- The manual explains a lawful allocation rather than changing binding law: The manual explains a lawful allocation rather than changing binding law, 1995-03-06. Kennedy’s opinion of the Court, Parts I–III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer. The same five expressly adopt interpretive status, the lawful actual ground, and the bounded remedy.

**Present operation effective:** 1995-03-06; the component authorities identified above.

**Exceptions and limits:** Actual incurred cost excludes unnecessary costs in efficient delivery of needed services; allocation must prevent cross-subsidy in either direction between Medicare and non-Medicare patients. Necessary-and-proper-interest and reasonable patient-care-cost limits remain. No universal amortization period, immediate-recognition right or general deference rule follows. Recall without new debt is outside §233’s advance-refunding rule. Necessary borrowing accounts for available sinking funds, and excess borrowing to buy depreciable assets accounts for existing funded depreciation. Ordinary net allowable refinancing costs cannot exceed costs without refinancing; compelling factors such as necessary cash flow or removal of a restrictive covenant may justify more upon a showing satisfactory to the intermediary. Without that showing the annual ceiling remains. No equity-capital award or later §1395hh rulemaking disposition is supplied.

**Controlling construction:** For advance refunding through new debt initiated on or after July 1, 1983, PRM §233 treats expense elements separately. New issue costs amortize from incurrence to new scheduled maturity; old unamortized discount, premium and issue costs run from new issuance until old holders receive principal, proportionately for serial bonds. Call premiums or penalties become allowable when old holders receive principal, prorated for serial payments. Interest on both debts is allowed annually as paid or accrued, with new discount/premium amortization adjusting interest and income from new proceeds offsetting it, including trust earnings. Redemption, miscellaneous expenses and annual authority/trustee fees retain their independent §2305 qualification; its exact allowance conditions are not established by this decision and no dependent entitlement is decided.

**Holdings navigation:** The accounting regulations do not compel immediate recognition of this loss; The manual explains a lawful allocation rather than changing binding law.

## Standards and Tests selection: Controlling weight for a responsible agency's fair regulatory interpretation

### Controlling weight for a responsible agency's fair regulatory interpretation

**Governing question:** Judicial treatment of a responsible agency's interpretation of its own regulation, including a construction presented in an amicus brief.

**Current rule:** The responsible agency's fair and considered interpretation controls unless plainly erroneous or inconsistent with the regulation. Courts must assess the reading against the operative text, lawful allocation of interpretive responsibility and supported agency treatment. An amicus presentation neither disqualifies the construction nor establishes its correctness. Interpretive authority does not permit amendment of contrary text, unsupported litigation rationalization, automatic authority for an agency lawyer or deference to unproved employer-specific factual assertions.

**Authority by component:**

- Auer v. Robbins — The Secretary's fair and considered regulatory interpretation controls within its legal bounds, 1997-02-19; Kennedy Part III, joined by all seven other Associates (eight); Stone-Zsela's replacement proposal is noncontrolling

**Present operation effective:** Auer, 1997-02-19, for the amicus and factual-assertion boundaries.

**Related current rules:** Authoritative interpretive Guidelines commentary preserves its separate statutory and constitutional limits. Medicare educational costs shifted from a related institution remains the distinct substantive reimbursement rule.

**Holdings navigation:** Auer v. Robbins (1997-02-19) — The Secretary's fair and considered regulatory interpretation controls within its legal bounds.

## Standards and Tests selection: Recognition of state representative-settlement judgments

### Recognition of state representative-settlement judgments

**Governing question:** A state class-settlement judgment releasing claims within exclusive federal adjudicatory jurisdiction.

**Current rule:** Section 1738 first requires the effect the rendering State gives its judgment; class form, consent disposition and exclusive federal jurisdiction alone create no categorical exception. Approval of an authorized representative release differs from adjudicating the released federal liability. Exchange Act §27 does not impliedly partially repeal §1738 for this judgment category. An incorporated, affirmed Delaware release has its ordinary state-law effect subject to Delaware's identical-factual-predicate and representative limits; alleged ordinary state-law error does not itself reopen that judgment. Recognition remains subject to constitutional authority to bind absent persons and does not confer state jurisdiction over federal securities liability or authorize coercive interference with federal litigation.

**Authority by component:**

- State-law-first framework: Matsushita — Section 1738 governs this kind of judgment, February 27, 1996; unanimous Part I.
- Ordinary Delaware recognition: the same Record, “This judgment receives ordinary Delaware preclusive effect,” Part II, seven Justices (all except Stevens and Ginsburg).
- Section 27 nonrepeal: the same Record, “Section 27 does not impliedly partially repeal §1738 here,” Part III, eight Justices (all except Ginsburg).

**Present operation effective:** Matsushita, February 27, 1996.

**Open question:** Actual constitutional adequacy, lawful binding and the complete permissible scope of collateral review, including effect of a full and fair prior adequacy determination, remain unresolved. No universal same-transaction release rule or fiction of individually negotiated consent arises from failure to opt out; valid opt-outs remain outside the release.

**Related current rules:** Constitutional authority to bind absent litigants supplies the independent constitutional minimum.

**Holdings navigation:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367.

## Standards and Tests selection: Constitutional authority to bind absent litigants

### Constitutional authority to bind absent litigants

**Governing question:** The due-process prerequisite to claim or issue preclusion against a nonparty.

**Current rule:** Identical interests, vigorous advocacy, resources, public importance and fiscal reliance alone do not authorize binding absent persons' own legal rights. Binding requires constitutionally sufficient consent, control, genuine privity, adequate class or representative litigation, or another recognized relationship or special remedial arrangement consistent with due process. Constitutionally inadequate representation is not independently cured by notice, opportunities to object or opt out; notice alone imposes no general duty on strangers to intervene. Issue preclusion, like claim preclusion, requires a lawful basis to bind the person. Genuine representative public-right adjudication and use of precedent in a later case remain distinct from extinguishing a stranger's personal claim.

**Authority by component:**

- Representation independent of notice and exclusion rights: Matsushita — Constitutional representation and unresolved review questions limit the remand, February 27, 1996; unanimous Part IV-A; eight Associates reserve complete collateral-review scope in IV-B.
- Nonparty binding and direct personal tax claims, including the issue-preclusion alternative: Richards — Holdings II–IV, June 10, 1996; Stone-Zsela for all nine.

**Present operation effective:** Richards, June 10, 1996; Matsushita supplies the retained representation component.

**Open question:** The Court assumes without deciding whether adequate representation may cure absent notice in some representative proceedings. It neither requires individual notice in every such action nor constitutionalizes every federal class-action requirement. The broader adequacy of officials' representation of constituents, ordinary state-law-error review and Matsushita's collateral-review boundary are not resolved.

**Holdings navigation:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367; Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793.

## Standards and Tests selection: Diversity cured before trial after erroneous removal

### Diversity cured before trial after erroneous removal

**Governing question:** Effect of a preserved removal error when complete diversity actually exists before trial and through judgment.

**Current rule:** Absent complete diversity at removal, a timely remand motion requires remand under the applicable statutes. A later lawful dismissal of the nondiverse party does not validate the earlier refusal, waive the objection or require extraordinary pretrial review. If actual §1332 diversity arises before trial and continues through judgment, that earlier defect alone does not require vacating the completed federal judgment. Actual jurisdiction at trial and judgment is indispensable; consent, efficiency and finality cannot supply it.

**Authority by component:**

- Earlier statutory duty: Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis — Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis — The erroneous refusal to remand remains error; bounded judgment-preservation rule: Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis — Actual jurisdiction before trial preserves the completed judgment against this earlier defect. O’Connor, all nine Justices, December 10, 1996.

**Present operation effective:** Caterpillar, December 10, 1996.

**Exceptions and limits:** No universal cure for removal defects, collusive diversity manufacture or collateral review of an entered covered remand is authorized. Independently available fees, sanctions and ordinary merits appeals retain their own requirements.

**Holdings navigation:** Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis (1996-12-10) — The erroneous refusal to remand remains error; Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis (1996-12-10) — Actual jurisdiction before trial preserves the completed judgment against this earlier defect.

## Standards and Tests selection: Ancillary enforcement and a new judgment debtor

### Ancillary enforcement and a new judgment debtor

**Governing question:** Whether an existing federal judgment supplies jurisdiction over a later independent action imposing its debt on another person.

**Current rule:** The court's power to identify, preserve and execute against its judgment debtor's assets does not confer jurisdiction over a later independent action imposing that debtor's full obligation on another person. Such an action needs an independent jurisdictional basis; factual connection, remedial need, Rule 69 and a supplemental-jurisdiction claim lacking an original federal anchor cannot supply it. The decree-effectuation branch of ancillary jurisdiction remains distinct from factual interdependence and from creation of a new obligation.

**Authority by component:**

- New-debtor limit and preserved enforcement: Peacock — Ancillary jurisdiction does not support this new full-debt action; The jurisdictional disposition preserves ordinary enforcement and leaves unresolved merits questions, February 21, 1996; Thomas for Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer and himself (eight).

**Present operation effective:** Peacock, February 21, 1996.

**Exceptions and limits:** Ordinary contempt, asset discovery, attachment, garnishment and fraudulent-transfer process directed to debtor property remain available on their own lawful predicates, as does an independently founded action. There is no general bar on every alter-ego proceeding or every proceeding involving a nondebtor, and ancillary subject-matter jurisdiction does not itself authorize nationwide service.

**Related current rules:** Jurisdiction to enforce a settlement after dismissal states Kokkonen's distinct order-incorporation and retained-jurisdiction mechanics.

**Holdings navigation:** Peacock v. Thomas, 516 U.S. 349.

## Standards and Tests selection: Concrete personal interests and procedural citizen-suit standing

### Concrete personal interests and procedural citizen-suit standing

**Governing question:** Article III standing for a citizen-suit plaintiff alleging a deprivation of procedure, including ESA §11(g) enforcement.

**Current rule:** A plaintiff invoking federal jurisdiction must establish a concrete and particularized, actual or imminent injury; a causal connection fairly traceable to the defendant's challenged conduct rather than to independent third parties' own decisions; and a likelihood, not mere speculation, that the requested relief will redress the injury. Authorization for any person to sue does not supply standing for only the public's undifferentiated interest in lawful administration. A procedural right must protect the plaintiff's own threatened concrete interest; when that connection exists, the plaintiff need not prove that the required procedure will certainly alter the ultimate agency decision. Aesthetic, recreational or professional wildlife interests may qualify, but past visits and indefinite return intentions without concrete plans or another supported basis for imminent renewed exposure do not establish threatened interference with personal use. At summary judgment the plaintiff must support challenged standing elements with specific facts by affidavit or other evidence, with favorable reasonable inferences available; allegations cannot replace missing evidence.

**Authority by component:**
- Stage-specific evidentiary requirement and existing injury, traceability and redressability elements: Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, “1. Article III elements must be supported at the litigation stage”, June 12, 1992; Scalia Part II, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas (five).
- Prospective personal-use boundary: Lujan, “2. Indefinite return intentions and general species interests do not establish threatened personal injury here,” June 12, 1992; Scalia Part III-A, same five-Justice opinion; Stone separately supports the record-specific conclusion.
- Citizen-suit and procedural-right boundary: Lujan, “3. Citizen-suit authorization does not dispense with a threatened concrete interest,” June 12, 1992; Scalia Part IV, same five Justices.

**Present operation effective:** Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, June 12, 1992, for the identified citizen-suit and procedural-right clarification; ordinary Article III elements and evidentiary burdens are retained.

**Exceptions and limits:** No purchased-ticket or particular-document requirement is adopted. The redressability application to absent agencies, binding force of the challenged regulation upon those agencies, effect of particular funding changes and ESA §7(a)(2)'s foreign reach are not controlling holdings.

**Holdings navigation:** Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, 504 U.S. 555 (1992) — Standing and personal injury.

## Standards and Tests selection: Endangered Species Act citizen-suit channels

### Endangered Species Act citizen-suit channels

**Governing question:** Scope of 16 U.S.C. §1540(g) citizen suits and their notice and enforcement conditions.

**Current rule:** The authorization of any person removes a separate species-preservation zone-of-interests restriction within the channels Congress actually authorized; Article III injury remains necessary. Section (g)(1)(C) reaches failure to perform a nondiscretionary §1533 duty, including a required consideration of economic effects of an alleged habitat designation. Section (g)(1)(A) does not authorize general supervision of alleged §1536 maladministration. When a habitat designation or revision actually occurs, §1533(b)(2) requires consideration of economic and other relevant impacts; exclusion is permitted if its benefits outweigh inclusion, but forbidden if the best scientific and commercial data show nondesignation will cause extinction.

**Authority by component:**

- Bennett v. Spear — The citizen-suit language is broad within its actual channels, 1997-03-19; unanimous Opinion of the Court supplies the channel-specific reach and preserved §1533(b)(2) conditions

**Present operation effective:** Bennett, 1997-03-19.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The (A) route generally requires 60 days’ written notice to the Secretary and alleged violator; a commenced civil-penalty action or a commenced, diligently prosecuted United States criminal action addressing the violation bars it. The (B) route separately requires 60 days’ notice explaining the claimed emergency and is barred by the Secretary’s commenced diligent emergency-determination action. The (C) route requires 60 days’ notice to the Secretary, except that suit concerning an emergency posing significant risk to the well-being of a fish, wildlife or plant species may follow notification immediately.

**Exceptions and limits:** A threshold review route establishes neither that a designation occurred nor that a nondiscretionary duty was triggered, violated or must lead to a particular exclusion.

**Holdings navigation:** Bennett v. Spear (1997-03-19) — Environmental Law / Endangered Species Act citizen-suit channels.

## Standards and Tests selection: APA review of completed species-consultation determinations

### APA review of completed species-consultation determinations

**Governing question:** APA review and finality of completed §1536 consultation determinations.

**Current rule:** The ESA does not itself preclude APA review of administrative consultation claims for which its citizen-suit channels supply no other adequate judicial remedy. Claimed injury from restrictions unsupported by the best scientific and commercial data is at least arguably within §1536(a)(2)'s protected interests. A Biological Opinion is final agency action when it completes the Service's consultation determination and an incidental-take statement carries legal consequences by conditioning protection from taking liability on compliance. The operating agency's retained discretion does not by itself turn that completed, consequential determination into tentative advice.

**Authority by component:**

- Bennett v. Spear — The consultation claims have an APA route and a provision-specific interest, 1997-03-19; unanimous Opinion of the Court supplies the APA route and provision-specific interests
- Bennett v. Spear — The Biological Opinion is completed action with legal consequences, 1997-03-19; the same all-nine coalition supplies consultation finality and conditional legal consequences

**Present operation effective:** Bennett, 1997-03-19.

**Exceptions and limits:** Section 702 addresses relief other than money damages and preserves other legal and equitable limits and relief forbidden by another consent-to-suit statute. Any mandatory or injunctive decree must identify by name or title the responsible federal officers and their successors. Section 704 requires final action and no other adequate court remedy.

**Controlling construction:** Section 1536(a)(2) requires avoidance of likely jeopardy and destruction or adverse modification of designated critical habitat using the best scientific and commercial data available, unless the Committee has granted an exemption under (h). Under (b)(4), the Secretary must conclude that the action will not violate (a)(2), or offer reasonable and prudent alternatives believed not to violate it, and conclude that incidental taking will not violate that subsection. For a marine mammal, taking also requires §1371(a)(5) authorization. The resulting statement specifies the taking’s impact, reasonable and prudent minimization measures, any required marine-mammal measures, and implementing terms including reporting. Section (o)(2) protects only taking that complies with those terms; it is not unconditional immunity from every ESA duty. The separate exemption-based protection in (o)(1) is not the source of this statement’s effect.

**Open question:** The statutory and scientific merits, actual designation, triggered duty and ultimate relief remain for adjudication.

**Related current rules:** APA limits on exhaustion of superior-agency appeals states the independent §704 finality and required/inoperative-agency-appeal rule; Equitable restraint of officers acting beyond statutory authority addresses the separate non-APA officer remedy.

**Holdings navigation:** Bennett v. Spear (1997-03-19) — Administrative Law / Review of completed biological consultation; Bennett v. Spear (1997-03-19) — Whether the Biological Opinion completes agency action with legal consequences.

## Standards and Tests selection: Limited modification authority and mandatory tariff filing

### Limited modification authority and mandatory tariff filing

**Governing question:** Whether the Communications Act’s power to modify filing requirements permits eliminating them for an entire class.

**Current rule:** Section 203(b)(2) permits the Commission, in its discretion and for good cause shown, to modify requirements made by or under §203 in particular instances or by general order applicable to special circumstances or conditions; no required notice period may exceed 120 days. That authority permits adjustments to filing form, contents, timing or place, including consequential and class-wide technical changes, but not replacement of mandatory filing throughout the nondominant long-distance sector with an optional system. The inquiry examines the obligation and whether its essential operation is adjusted or removed, not a numerical market-share threshold. Good cause alone cannot authorize the replacement. The Act’s exception for connecting carriers preserves their separate inspection duties; §203(c)’s allowance for otherwise lawful authority does not confer unlimited dispensing power.

**Authority by component:**

- Bounded modification and distinct notice authority: MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co — The modification power does not permit this replacement of mandatory filing, 1994-06-17. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela's Opinion of the Court, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg. Five of eight participating Justices directly adopt the structural modification ground, its application and its stated limits; no aggregation of separate rationales is necessary.

**Present operation effective:** MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co, 1994-06-17.

**Exceptions and limits:** Limited deferral or waiver is not categorically foreclosed, but no particular waiver is approved. Failure of the special-circumstances-or-conditions predicate is a four-Justice alternative, not a separate controlling holding. No universal major-questions doctrine is created; the statute’s permissible flexibility remains available within the actual delegation.

**Holdings navigation:** The modification power does not permit this replacement of mandatory filing.

## Standards and Tests selection: National-bank annuity brokerage and §92 classification

### National-bank annuity brokerage and §92 classification

**Governing question:** The banking power and insurance provision as applied to conditioned annuity agency sales.

**Current rule:** The business of banking under 12 U.S.C. §24 Seventh is not exhausted by its listed activities. The Comptroller may reasonably include agency sales of fixed, variable and combined annuities as financial-intermediary investment activity, within real banking and incidental-power bounds and express statutory restrictions. Reasonable classification of those products as investments outside §92’s insurance category sustains the reviewed brokerage permission; it authorizes no underwriting, issuance or guarantee of an insurer’s obligations or unrelated commercial enterprise.

**Authority by component:**

- Reasoned approval of annuity brokerage falls within the banking power: Reasoned approval of annuity brokerage falls within the banking power, 1995-01-18. Justice Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Eight of nine participating Justices directly adopt this rule and its agency-review rationale. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in both judgments on an independent statutory ground and does not join this opinion.
- Section 92 does not defeat this annuity classification: Section 92 does not defeat this annuity classification, 1995-01-18. Justice Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Eight of nine participating Justices directly adopt this rule and its agency-review rationale. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in both judgments on an independent statutory ground and does not join this opinion.

**Present operation effective:** 1995-01-18; the component authorities identified above.

**Open question:** Whether §92 exclusively limits genuine insurance-agency activity to qualifying small places, distinct state insurance classifications and state-law preemption remain undecided. When §92 applies, its small-place conditions and prohibitions on guaranteeing premiums or the truth of statements by the assured remain operative; classification here does not repeal them.

**Holdings navigation:** Reasoned approval of annuity brokerage falls within the banking power; Section 92 does not defeat this annuity classification.

## Standards and Tests selection: Prosecutorial investigation, advocacy and press statements

### Prosecutorial investigation, advocacy and press statements

**Governing question:** What instructions govern prosecutorial investigation, advocacy and press statements?

**Current rule:** Ordinary investigative work to identify a perpetrator before probable cause has no absolute prosecutorial immunity for alleged fabrication; later courtroom use does not retrospectively transform it into advocacy. Genuine evaluation and preparation in an advocate's role remain protected; neither all pre-indictment nor all post-indictment acts share one classification. Statements to the press announcing an indictment also lack absolute prosecutorial immunity merely because they concern prosecution or cause injury through subsequent proceedings. Qualified immunity, an actionable constitutional deprivation and other liability predicates remain separate.

Preparing and filing charging papers or a warrant motion, evaluating evidence for those submissions and presenting the State's legal case receive absolute immunity when they are advocacy functions. Bad purpose or alleged false evidence does not remove that protection; the Court adopts no general dishonesty exception. A prosecutor's distinct personal oath supplying facts to establish probable cause for an arrest warrant is instead a complaining-witness evidentiary function without absolute prosecutorial immunity. That classification does not require proof of falsity and leaves ordinary courtroom testimony immunity intact. Denial of this immunity does not establish liability: constitutional deprivation, culpability, materiality, causation and compensable injury and available personal defenses remain separate. A judge's warrant does not categorically sever causation where material deception procured it, while a truthful corrected account may still establish probable cause.

**Authority by component:**

- Investigative fabrication is not protected by absolute prosecutorial immunity: Buckley v. Fitzsimmons — Investigative fabrication is not protected by absolute prosecutorial immunity, 1993-06-24. Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.
- A press conference does not carry an advocate’s absolute immunity: Buckley v. Fitzsimmons — A press conference does not carry an advocate’s absolute immunity, 1993-06-24. Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices).
